# Retractions

This file records every published statement in this repository that a later
revision **withdrew or inverted**. A clarification that only adds detail is not
listed here; a statement an implementer could already have built against, and
which is now wrong, is.

The convention exists because this repository's corrections have historically
arrived inside otherwise-routine edits, where a reader who had already
implemented the earlier text had no reason to re-read the section. Each row
names the affected section, and each affected section also carries a one-line
inline note at the point of the change.

## What earns a row

A **reversal**: the specification previously published a statement, and the
current text says something incompatible with it, so an implementation built
faithfully on the old text now produces wrong behaviour. Inverted polarity,
transposed fields, a relocated or re-identified byte, a withdrawn subsystem, a
changed numeric contract, a permission to omit that is now load-bearing, and a
negative claim that turned positive all qualify.

A **clarification** does not earn a row: added detail, a narrowed scope that
leaves the old text still true, a gap being filled for the first time, a
renaming with no behavioural consequence, or a first publication of geometry or
constants the spec had previously declined to state. The bar is deliberately
high. A bloated index with false rows is nearly as bad as no index, because
consumers stop trusting it and re-audit sections that never changed.

Statements published **only in an issue closing comment** and never in spec
prose still earn a row, because issue answers are this project's delivery
channel to the implementation. Those rows name the issue instead of a commit.

## How to use it

1. Note the spec commit your implementation last reconciled against.
2. Read every row at or after that commit in the table below. Rows are in spec
   commit order, oldest first, and the file is append-only, so new rows always
   arrive at the bottom.
3. Re-audit exactly the named sections. Do not re-read the whole document.
4. Treat a passing test as evidence of nothing on those sections. The failure
   mode this index exists for is a test that pins retracted behaviour and keeps
   passing.

Several commits below are work-in-progress snapshots that were finalised by the
repair commit immediately following them. Where the cited commit is a snapshot,
the published form may have landed in its successor:
`a97b490` -> `55a5f09`, `e0b73e1` -> `31d331c`, `46df660` -> `3ca17f0`,
`1a8be19` -> `242bc2a`, `4ae70dc` -> `d80c16b`.

## Completeness caveat

**This index is a backfill, and it is not proven exhaustive.** It was assembled
in one pass over 192 commits of history by three independent sweeps, each with
known blind spots:

- **Retraction-vocabulary sweep of current prose.** Every occurrence of
  retract / withdraw / supersede / "earlier revision" / "was wrong" in
  `systems/`, `formats/` and `catalogs/` at HEAD (423 hits across 70
  documents), attributed to its introducing commit by `git blame` and read as
  353 distinct paragraphs. This finds reversals that the corrected text was
  honest about. It cannot find a reversal that left no note.
- **Removed-line sweep of the full patch history.** Every line removed across
  all 192 commits that no longer appears at HEAD (3,911 lines), filtered for
  normative absolutes and for polarity vocabulary, then read. This is the sweep
  that found the *silent* reversals, including the `npc-schedules.md` tile-set
  bit polarity and the Frigate hull value. It is a keyword filter, so a short,
  line-wrapped removal using none of those words is unexamined. Six commits
  were excluded from this sweep by filter: `5b816cc`, `e3c6a0e` and `14d7cc0`
  (early wholesale authoring passes), and `0af01c2`, `c2b8269` and `5b0edb8`
  (provenance-redaction passes assumed semantically neutral). Reversals
  introduced **by** those six against still-earlier text are not covered.
- **Issue sweep.** All 149 issues were keyword-scanned; roughly 45 were read in
  full. An issue-channel reversal phrased without retraction vocabulary — a
  table quietly republished with different values — would be missed. Eighty
  issues were scanned but not read.

Other known gaps: no commit's full diff was read end to end, so a reversal
expressed only as a wholesale paragraph rewrite sharing no vocabulary with its
predecessor would escape all three sweeps. `EXTRACTION.md` was not mined and is
known to still carry at least one retracted name (the LOOKOBJ "Britannia
chunk-map overlay", withdrawn at `a97b490`). Rows whose withdrawn text lived
only in a private analysis note, never in published prose or an issue answer,
were deliberately excluded even where the current text says "an earlier
revision"; those notes are self-descriptions of an internal pipeline, not
contracts a consumer could have implemented.

**This index has already failed once, in exactly the way it exists to prevent.**
The mining pass that assembled it verified 425 reversals; the publish step that
produced the first form of this table wrote 230 rows. Part of that shortfall was
legitimate — many rows deliberately bundle several documents that shared one
reversal into a single row, and roughly thirty mined candidates were rejected as
clarifications rather than reversals — but part of it was not. A mechanical diff
of verified reversals against published rows flagged 49 with no apparent row at
all. A reconciliation pass re-checked all 49: 48 were already covered, by a
bundled row or by a row filed under a different section number, and **one was
genuinely omitted** — the issue-#42 dungeon-mode statement, now filed against
`systems/visibility.md` 12.5. None of the 49 was rejected as a clarification and
none was left undetermined. Appending that row brings the table to 231 rows.

Two things follow for a reader. First, the append-only, complete-for-reversals
contract stated at the head of this file is the intent of the file, not a proven
property of it: a section this index does not name has not thereby been cleared,
and at least one confirmed reversal did reach consumers unlisted. Second, the
single omission was one of three retractions carried by a single issue answer,
two of which were filed and the third of which was dropped — so the likeliest
remaining defect has the same shape: a multi-part correction filed for some of
its parts only. When re-auditing against a row that cites an issue, read that
whole issue answer, not only the part the row quotes.

**This index has now failed a second time, in a different way: reversals
retracted *inline* but never indexed.** The consuming engine found it, not us.
It had instructed every one of its audit agents to grep this index as their
single retraction check, on the strength of the "append-only and complete for
reversals" contract at the head of this file. That check was weaker than
presented. A section can carry a plain inline retraction — "any earlier
statement that X is retracted", "that is withdrawn", "**Correction:**" — and
have no row here at all, and an agent grepping only the index never sees it.
The engine named two: `systems/town-mode.md` section 17's poison-gas cadence
bullet, and `systems/blackthorn.md` section 5's withdrawal of the "punishment
animation / dragged-away victim" wording, whose sibling withdrawal in section 4
*was* filed, as R205.

A five-part sweep followed, reading every document in `systems/` (51),
`formats/` (26) and `catalogs/` (9), plus `EXTRACTION.md`, `NEXT-STEPS.md` and
`README.md` — 89 documents — with two or three independent retraction-vocabulary
passes over every line, each hit mapped to its enclosing section and checked
against the Document and Section columns of every row. It found **39 reversals
this index did not usably carry**, now filed as **R233 through R271**:

- **26** had no row anywhere. Two systemic shapes recur. Commit `072ff40` had
  zero rows despite its own message declaring "Two corrections to previously
  published text" (R233, R234). And the multi-part-correction failure this
  caveat already predicted happened twice more: R235 is the dropped section-5
  half of the `e5ce960` correction whose other parts are R203, R204, R205 and
  R208, and R247 is the dropped second half of the two-part withdrawal whose
  first half is R074.
- **13** were reversals this index *did* record, but under a document or a
  section number the affected document's own grep could not reach — the whole
  reversal filed against a sibling document (R243, R244, R257, R258, R260,
  R261, R264, R268, R270), against a sibling section (R262, R263), or against
  one half of a paragraph (R259, R267). `formats/shoppe-dat.md` appeared zero
  times in this file before R260. R262 exposes a plain pointer error: R109's
  Section column reads "npc 9" and the retraction is in section 8, which was
  section 8 at R109's own commit too.

Roughly sixty further sites carrying retraction vocabulary were read and
deliberately given **no** row: about half were already covered by an existing
row at the named document and section, and the rest were clarifications,
renamings with no behavioural consequence, or supersessions of readings that
lived only in a private analysis note. Those judgements are recorded in the
sweep, not here, so that this table does not bloat with false rows.

Three limits on that sweep, stated plainly. It was a vocabulary sweep of
current prose, so a **silent** reversal — a paragraph rewritten wholesale with
no note — is outside what it could find; no removed-line diff sweep was run for
it. The issue tracker could not be read from the sweep's workspace, so four
findings (R240 and R253 through R255) rest on the spec's own self-description
that the withdrawn text was delivered, and are filed against the issue rather
than a commit. And section attribution comes from the nearest preceding
heading.

What a reader should take from this: **the index is a necessary check, not a
sufficient one.** Grepping this file for your document tells you which
reversals were *filed* against it. Until every inline retraction in the spec is
also a row — which is now the stated convention in `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md`,
where an inline-only retraction is named as a bug — a grep of a section's own
prose for retraction vocabulary remains a second check worth running, and the
two together are stronger than either.

If you find a reversal that is not listed, it is a defect in this file. Open an
issue and it will be appended.

## Index

## Row identifiers and the audited-through protocol

Every row carries a permanent identifier in the `Row` column, `R001` upward.
**An identifier is never reused and never renumbered**, including if a row is
later found to be wrong: a withdrawn row keeps its id and gains a note saying
so. New rows take the next free number, so the highest id in the file is also
the row count.

Ids exist so a consumer can record a watermark rather than re-reading the
table. The intended loop:

1. Audit the rows you have not yet audited.
2. Record the highest id you covered, for example `audited clean through R231`.
3. On the next pass, read only rows above that id.

The `Spec commit` column is not a safe watermark on its own. Rows are ordered
by the commit that made the change, but a reversal can be discovered and filed
long after the commit that caused it, so a row with an older commit may be
appended below a newer one. Ids are append-ordered and monotonic; commits are
not. Use the id.

Two caveats a consumer should encode rather than assume. A row may be edited in
place to correct its wording without its id changing, so re-reading an
already-audited row is cheap insurance if the file has changed at all. And the
completeness caveat below applies to the whole table: an id watermark tells you
what you have read, not that everything worth reading has been written.

| R082 | `55a5f09` | `systems/display-driver-abi.md`, `systems/display-driver.md` | display-driver-abi 6; display-driver 8 | Dispatch offset `0x3F` is a clipped **front-buffer only** rectangle fill: with the back buffer selected it skips its per-scanline fill, back-buffer fills are owned by offsets `0x39` and `0x3C`, and compatibility tests should encode `0x3F` as front-buffer. | All three statements are withdrawn. | R083 | `55a5f09` | `systems/display-driver-abi.md` | 9.6 | The rectangle dissolve's speaker effect is per-pixel with a pitch that tracks progress through the rectangle (issue #53); and the resident core wraps the entry in an outer loop that re-invokes the world tick every few visits, so the dissolve does not freeze gameplay timing. | Both halves are withdrawn. The strip is wide and short, `304 x 64` pixels. A reader that transposes it places every cell wrongly. |
| R086 | `55a5f09` | `systems/intro.md`, `formats/location-dat.md` | intro 12; location-dat 11 | Two successive states: the strip starts from its **middle row** and expands outward over animation ticks; then, at `5fe00f0`, that reveal was itself retracted as "stale, non-normative prose" and command `0x06` was said to load the strip in one script action with no reveal mask required. | The retraction of the reveal is itself withdrawn: the reveal is real and normative, and only its axis was wrong. The strip opens outward from its centre **column** — the cursor starts on column 9 and widens one column each side every second tick, reaching the full `0..18` at the end of the eighteenth tick. All four rows of a revealed column appear together. |
| R087 | `55a5f09` | `systems/intro.md` | 12 | The preview renderer switches to a Return-to-View display state and configures the text rectangles used by the chapter captions. | Neither is accurate. | R088 | `55a5f09` | `systems/intro.md` | 10.1 step 6 | Both inline doorway lines are 44 characters long. | Each is exactly 45 characters, stored as a 45-byte string plus a one-byte NUL. A fixed 44-byte buffer or length assertion truncates the final character. |
| R089 | `55a5f09` | `systems/intro.md` | 11.1, 11.3 | The acknowledgements handler never selects a font, never sets a text rectangle, never emits a printable character and never changes a palette or text attribute; and **no pixel above row `63` is written on either surface at any point**. | Both blanket claims are withdrawn. | R198 | `d80c16b` | `systems/shops.md` | 8.A | The live-dialogue table's columns are "shared preamble records" and "initial-greeting records", with a shared initial greeting drawn from four `SHOPPE.DAT` records. | Only the first column is entry text. The second and third columns are both **exit** rows, rendered by one shared closing-bark step at the end of a visit and selected by a transaction-outcome code: exit with nothing bought, and exit with a purchase completed. |
| R199 | `8ce0b2c` | `systems/overworld.md` | 9, 9.1 | The gate-presence counter's lifetime, published in the closing comments on issue #86: first that it ticks in per-turn cleanup, then that it is a short-lived animation counter whose whole lifecycle sits inside a single call. | **Persistent, not turn-scoped and not call-scoped.** There is exactly one such byte, it is save-backed, and it survives turns, mode changes, scene changes and save/load alike. | R200 | `8ce0b2c` | `systems/containers.md` | 9 | The plague outcome for a corpse search clears the corpse slot without staging food or gold; non-plague corpse searches rewrite the slot into a later food/gold pickup. | Backwards. | R201 | `fa9794b` | `catalogs/tile-catalog.md` | 4, 11 | Two successive readings of moongate rendering: first a bespoke render-frame plate driven by an overworld moongate animator cycling a sixteen-step visual sequence with "Effect 448..495 moongate frames"; then, after that was withdrawn, that moongate graphics are **not animated at all** and the renderer paints a gate like any other tile. | The first withdrawal overshot. Moongates have no authored frame family and are not members of any animator cycle, but a gate cell is painted as a plain tile only at **full presence**: at every intermediate phase the renderer *composes* the frame at draw time from the gate tile and the cell's ground tile into scratch tile `0x116`. Phase is a save-backed presence value. |
| R202 | `b04a2e5` | `systems/active-objects.md` | 8.1 | Off-screen pruning releases slots through the ordinary slot-freeing rule of section 4 — a one-byte write, no free list, no compaction. | The prune path calls the shared record-writer with the slot index and **six zero field values**, clearing the record's first six bytes rather than only its type byte. Section 4's one-byte rule remains correct for the other paths that free slots. (Low blast radius: the allocator's initialiser rewrites the same six bytes.) |
| R203 | `e5ce960` | `systems/screen-mode-dispatch.md`, `systems/disk-prompt.md`, `formats/data-ovl.md`, `systems/launcher.md`, `systems/save-load.md`, `systems/display-driver-abi.md` | screen-mode-dispatch (whole document); data-ovl 6.4, 6.5; launcher 10; save-load 7.2; display-driver-abi 2 | A resident screen / prompt presentation controller, driven by a presentation-mode byte indexing a per-mode state table of setup and prompt branches, with mode-setup and mode-query helpers, a low-bit presentation toggle, and the disk prompt as one narrow sub-case. | **The document is withdrawn in full: its subject did not exist.** The routine is the disk-insert / disk-error handler. Replacement: `systems/disk-prompt.md`. |
| R204 | `e5ce960` | `systems/blackthorn.md` | 3, 8 | During the opening capture pass the handler scans all eight party slots for the first whose **per-member Blackthorn-jail flag** is clear, skipping members already marked jailed; the state table lists jailed/handled party-member flags as durable story state. | There is no per-member jail flag. The eight bytes are the game's durable **shrine ruin flags**, already documented in `formats/saved-gam.md` and `systems/quest-flags.md`. The eight-slot scan selects a **shrine** — the first that is neither ruined nor restored — not a party member. |
| R205 | `e5ce960` | `systems/blackthorn.md` | 4 | Six claims: the answer lookup is indexed by prompt ordinal; only the first four ordinals are iterated; a correct answer marks the target party member jailed; the flow can silently route a member into jail state; the challenge does not adjust numeric karma; and the loop's party-slot argument names which companion is at risk. | The loop asks about **one shrine, up to four times**; the shrine index is fixed before the loop and the four ordinals change only the wording. A correct answer ruins that shrine, costs five points of moral standing, and **kills** one companion as "a merciful death". |

| R207 | `e5ce960` | `systems/town-mode.md` | 13, 14 | The Shadowlord entry sweep puts every eligible NPC into a **fortified/alert** state for Hatred and a **pacified** state for Cowardice; a non-attack event from an alarmed NPC can pacify it. | "Fortified" was inverted — the mode installed is an **approach** mode — and "pacified" was wrong in the other direction: the NPC is **frightened**, not calmed. | R208 | `e5ce960` | `systems/overworld.md` | 9.2 step 1 | The moon-gate transit's opening speaker sweep comes from "the same generator the shrine effect uses". | The comparison is withdrawn — it rested on a mis-identified routine and the shrine effect does not use this helper at all. | R209 | `f9baac6` | `systems/conversation.md` | 7 | Bytes `0x80..0x9F`, outside the GOTO range, are engine control codes — in direct contradiction with the neighbouring bullet making `0x01..0x80` dictionary tokens. | The control band is `0x81..0x9F`. **`0x80` is a dictionary token, not a control code**, and neither band's boundary may be derived from the other. |
| R210 | `b3c1f4e` | `systems/inventory.md` | 5.1 | R-Ready's picker literals: `None!` is printed when the picker is cancelled and `Done` when it is closed after use. | The two are the wrong way round for R-Ready. `Done` is what R-Ready prints when the player leaves the picker with **Escape**; `None!` is printed by the *other* caller of the same shared picker on its Escape, never by R-Ready. The shared picker's single Escape arm chooses between them from the mode it was opened with. |

The live door and restraint identifiers are stocks `0x84`, manacles `0x85`, magic locks `0x97`/`0x98`, the door pairs `0xB8`/`0xB9` and `0xBA`/`0xBB`, already-open `0xAF` and cleared cobble `0x44`. |

| R223 | `4565e85` | `systems/town-mode.md` | 13 | The harpsichord's ten keys are a **descending** major scale, digit `1` highest and digit `0` lowest. | The scale **ascends**: digit `1` is the lowest note, digit `8` is an octave above it, and `9` and `0` continue two whole tones further. See `audio.md` section 3 and `town-mode.md` section 13.1. |
| R225 | `4565e85` | `systems/audio.md` | 6 | Variant 6's confirmed uses include "Kill/Slay Living". | The combat-only attack spells play **no shared variant at all**. | R226 | `4565e85` | `systems/audio.md` | 7.3 | The **previous wind state** selects the variant: Calm-to-direction plays variant 2, direction-to-anything plays variant 1. | The selector is a **caller tag**, not the old wind. | R228 | `4565e85` | `systems/audio.md` | 7.4 | A rejected **town** movement and a rejected **combat** step-or-attack beep. | A rejected **overworld** step beeps too, with the identical recipe; the earlier sentence under-scoped by one mode. | R229 | `4565e85` | `systems/audio.md` | 8.1 | The ring-vanishes cue is skipped by "a cancelled confirmation". | There is no confirmation prompt. Destruction is a 1-in-16 random roll with no player interaction, on both the Ready path and the terrain-combat-entry path. See `audio.md` section 8.1. |
| R230 | `4565e85` | `systems/display-driver-abi.md` | 9.6 | On each checked visit the gated dissolve "emits one short percussive speaker click", and an abort leaves "the speaker silenced" as part of the per-click behaviour. | The speaker is enabled at the first click and **nothing disables it until the dissolve exits**. Each click *retunes* a continuously running square wave; it is one continuous waveform whose frequency is randomised at the click cadence, not a train of discrete clicks. The single silencing point is the dissolve's shared exit, reached by both abort and completion. See `audio.md` section 8.6.1. |
| R231 | `4565e85` | `systems/timing.md` | 4 | The start/menu logo reveal "has no wait of any kind in its inner loop" and is close to instantaneous on a modern host. | True of the ungated dissolve. The **first, gated** dissolve pays a short calibrated hold per click — roughly 50 to 60 microseconds on every second visited pixel — plus the retune, and is modelled at several seconds for the full-width rectangle. See `audio.md` section 8.6.1. |
| R232 | `a6e2622` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/item-list.md` | combat 11, shared to-hit helper; item-list shared to-hit summary | The shared to-hit helper "accepts the hit when that score beats a uniform random byte", so a larger `(attacker - defender + 30) / 2` score means a better chance to hit. | **The comparison runs the other way: the hit is accepted when the drawn value is at or above the score.** A larger score therefore means a *smaller* chance to hit, and the score reads as a difficulty number. An engine built on the old direction inverts the outcome of every ordinary melee and ranged/effect attack. Two consequences ride along and are published as limits, not contract: the attacker/defender labelling of the two operands feeding the score is now suspect and was not re-derived, and the random source was not re-located, so its range - and any hit percentage derived from it - remains unverified. |
| R233 | `072ff40` | `systems/audio.md` | 3 | The software envelope's muted arm is a **cost-matched silent timing loop**, so muting changes output but not cadence — published as the bullet "a software envelope follows a matched silent timing loop". | The silent arm matches the audible arm in structure and iteration count but **not** in cost per iteration: it omits the comparison and the speaker-control work and runs about **23 percent faster**. Envelope-driven scene lengths are therefore mute-dependent, and section 10 gives the resulting difference. Distinct from R224, which covers the harpsichord handler skipping the sound call and whose replacement text re-affirms that the iteration loop still runs. |
| R234 | `072ff40` | `systems/audio.md` | 5.4, 5.4.1 | The software envelope generator **changes the speaker pin directly in a calibrated software loop**. | The loop repeatedly connects and disconnects an already-running timer carrier from the speaker. It is a **gate on a carrier**, not a software flip of the pin, and the audible waveform is the gate pattern. A frontend that synthesises the envelope as a pin-toggle at the loop rate is about **four octaves** wrong. |
| R235 | `e5ce960` | `systems/blackthorn.md` | 5 | The failure reaction is a presentation sequence only — Blackthorn prints a reaction, runs a **punishment animation**, and names the party's second visible member as the **dragged-away victim** — ending at "wait for player acknowledgement before returning to the caller branch". No roster or save-state effect was stated, and section 8's state table listed none. | The punishment is a permanent **execution**. The victim is the second living party member; the routine erases their actor, lifts their roster record out of the party, compacts the remaining records, decrements the party count, and parks the lifted record in the last roster slot with a whereabouts value matching no location, so no inn and no rescue path can retrieve them. The effect survives save and reload. The same execution runs on the correct-answer branch whenever more than one companion is alive. R205 files the section-4 withdrawal from this same commit; this is the section-5 part that was dropped. |
| R236 | `a97b490` -> `55a5f09` | `systems/conversation.md` | 7.4 | Control-code table row `0x8A` = PANEL-NEWLINE, "mark a newline boundary that also flushes the party-panel state", listed alongside `0x8D` = LITERAL-NEWLINE; `0x89` omitted from the table entirely. | The literal-newline code is `0x8D` and **only** `0x8D`. `0x8A` is not a control code with newline-plus-panel-flush semantics and there is no panel flush; `0x8A` acquires newline meaning only *inside the word buffer*, because the printable-text path rewrites a `0x8D` to `0x8A` after control-code dispatch has already been passed. `0x89` exists. R033 withdraws `0x87`/`0x88` in the `formats/tlk.md` control table at the same commit; this row covers the conversation-side `0x8A`/`0x8D`/`0x89` part, which no row named. |
| R237 | `e0b73e1` -> `31d331c` | `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md` | 3.1 | The Jimmy Dexterity test "rolls a uniform `1..29` die" and compares it to the selected member's Dexterity, restated for the visible locked chest tile. | A uniform die with **thirty outcomes beginning at zero**; the attempt succeeds when Dexterity is strictly greater than the roll. Success chance is Dexterity / 30, clamped at both ends: Dexterity 0 never picks, Dexterity 30 or above always picks. The old range is off by one at the top of the curve and denies a maximum-Dexterity character the guaranteed success the original gives. R035, R036 and R037 cover other parts of this section and none states the die range. |
| R238 | `e0b73e1` -> `31d331c` | `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md` | 3.2 | "If the high bit was already clear, Jimmy prints the broken-key result and **returns without changing keys**." | A container whose lock/trap flag is already clear is not re-rolled: Jimmy prints the broken-key result and **consumes one key** for nothing, on both the surface and the dungeon path. Key counts drift from the original over play if the short-circuit is implemented as free. R038 names this section but covers the broken-lock state and the failure path's writes, not the already-unlocked short-circuit. |
| R239 | `e0b73e1` -> `31d331c` | `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md` | 2 | A locked-versus-closeable **chest-on-floor tile pair**, "structurally identical to the door pair": open success writes a fixed open-container tile, Jimmy success rolls a key-pick check. | Surface and town chests are not part of the locked/unlocked tile pairing. A chest is a per-map container **object** whose lock, trap and contents state lives on the object record; J-Jimmy never matches a chest through its tile cascade at all, and chests reach it only through the object scan. The object record, not a tile pair, is the authority for whether a container is locked. |
| R240 | `0d7e76c` | `systems/containers.md` | 9 | A corpse-search contents split of **one third against two thirds**, and a plague chance of **one in thirty-one**. Neither figure appears in any spec document at `0d7e76c^`, so both were delivered through the issue channel. | Every roll is over a **closed** range: the arm split is 1 in 8 to the minority arm; inside the minority arm, 1 in 4 food and 3 in 4 gold; plague on 1 in 32 inside the cleared arm. Each withdrawn figure was an inclusive/exclusive off-by-one. R200 names this section but cites the earlier commit `8ce0b2c` and covers a different claim (which arm clears the corpse slot), so a consumer whose watermark is at R200 never re-reads section 9. |
| R241 | `15a07ff` | `systems/chargen.md` | 4.1 | The name-prompt chrome is a **colour-1** fill and a **colour-3** horizontal rule, given as literal colour indices. | The two fills are issued in user-interface colour **slots** 2 and 1 respectively, which resolve to palette indices `1` and `15` on the sixteen-colour drivers and to `1` and `3` only on the low-colour pair. The `3` was the low-colour table value for the accent pen, not the colour the EGA baseline draws the rule in, so an engine on the old text draws the name-prompt divider rule in the wrong colour on every sixteen-colour driver. R141 and R167 are the other `15a07ff` colour rows and neither names chargen. |
| R242 | `8ce0b2c` | `systems/animation.md` | 8 | A moongate is a live terrain byte written and removed by the once-per-turn saved-slot refresh, "**with no frame cycle of its own**" — an unqualified published negative that an implementation could reasonably read as "the gate is a static sprite". | The gate has no entry in the section 6 family table, no frame selector and no advance from the animation tick, but it **is** drawn through a **sixteen-phase rise-and-sink composition** whose phase is a persisted counter advanced by the once-per-turn refresh and driven directly by a blocking transition (contract in `systems/overworld.md` 9.1, 9.2). An engine that renders a static moongate sprite is visibly wrong. R199 covers the same counter's *lifetime* for overworld 9/9.1 and does not name this document. |
| R243 | `a97b490` -> `55a5f09` | `systems/encounters.md` | 7 | Published in this document's own prose: "Towns and other location-mode maps do not run the random-encounter probe **and do not use the arena-encounter setup for ordinary hostile-NPC events** in the traced town overlay." | Towns **do** reach arena combat. The town overlay's live NPC-conflict chain, entered from A-Attack and from post-action cleanup, passes the target NPC's linked active-object slot to the same terrain-combat entry the overworld uses; the arena resolves to the cobble arena and the town-style override forces monster count to one unless the target is a Guard. Same reversal as **R070**, whose bundle names `systems/town-mode.md` 14 only; this row files it under the document that carried and withdrew the claim in its own words, so a grep for `systems/encounters.md` finds it. Noted separately as a prose defect: section 13's boundary bullet still contradicts section 7 at HEAD. |
| R244 | `a97b490` -> `55a5f09` | `systems/encounters.md` | 13 | The alarm's schedule-rewrite sweep, named in this document's boundary list as "alarm scatter" — a sweep that displaces NPCs. | The sweep **displaces no NPC and writes no coordinate**. Same reversal as **R183**, whose bundle names `systems/town-mode.md` 7.1 and 14 only. town-mode owns the normative contract and the encounters mention is a boundary-list pointer, but a grep of this index for `systems/encounters.md` previously returned nothing for it. |
| R245 | `b3c1f4e` | `systems/magic.md` | 6 (M-Mix), step 7 | The wrong-mix branch refreshes a **dedicated scratch target slot**, and when no Good-or-Poisoned member exists the trap-effect resolver is handed whatever **party index** that dedicated scratch slot last held. | The word is not dedicated scratch: it is a **resident word the world-coordinate bookkeeping also uses as a map origin**, so a stale value is most likely a **map coordinate** rather than a party index. The mixer supplies the slot itself and does not use the shared acting-member selection the container callers use. The poison helper ignores a slot at or above the party count; the single-slot damage effect has no such bound, so it can be handed a coordinate-sized value. Whether the mixer's overwrite also disturbs the party's world position is marked UNVERIFIED (`systems/traps.md` 4). No row named `systems/magic.md` section 6 before this one. |
| R246 | `b3c1f4e` | `systems/magic.md`, `catalogs/item-list.md` | magic 8 (Locate); item-list 8 (Sextant) | The shared sextant-style coordinate printer "ends the line with a trailing double-quote character and a newline", and this is presentation only; the Sextant row published only the intra-line punctuation and a closing newline, which leaves an implementer free to print label and coordinates on one line. | Each coordinate renders high-letter, apostrophe, low-letter, **double-quote** — so there are **two** double-quotes per reading, one per coordinate, the second being the first character of the comma-space separator. The printer also emits a newline **before** the coordinate pair as well as after it, because the caller's label carries no newline of its own, so the label always stands on its own line. Both halves are output contracts a transcript test pins, and both callers — the Locate spell and the Sextant item — are affected. |
| R247 | `242bc2a` (repairing `1a8be19`) | `systems/magic.md`, `systems/combat.md` | magic 8 (Conjure / Swarm / Summon); combat 6.1a, 9 | A creature stamped with the controlled bit is still treated as **hostile to the party** by the friend/foe resolver; the controlled bit changes only the actor's *attack*. | The controlled bit also changes the actor's **combat group**: the slot-to-group helper reads the bit as a team toggle and inverts it for monster-side slots, so a stamped creature lands in the **party's** group for the same-faction filter. The same flip applies to a Charm target. An engine on the old text lets party area spells and same-faction filters treat conjured or charmed creatures as enemies, the opposite of shipped behaviour. **R074 files the other half of this same two-part withdrawal** (summoned creatures taking their turn through the player-command path); this is the half that was dropped. **R074's half is itself now superseded by R354 (issue #185):** that dispatch reading was correct and has been restored; only this row's combat-group correction survives from the pair. |
| R248 | `31d331c` (repairing `e0b73e1`) | `systems/lighting.md` | 5, 6 | "Dungeon mode also runs a **dungeon-local torch/light upkeep hook** before drawing the first-person view", in addition to the shared per-turn decay. The dungeon overlay only **reads** the two light counters as a blackout gate; the shared per-turn cleanup performs all counter decay, established by a whole-binary scan for readers and writers of both counters. An engine carrying the second decay site burns torches and light spells at roughly double rate underground. The only surviving trace of the withdrawal was a Sources bullet, so a reader of section 5 or 6 saw nothing; section 5 now carries the inline note. R018, R057, R107 and R113 are all filed against lighting 3 and none reaches this. |
| R249 | `1a8be19` -> `242bc2a` | `systems/intro.md`, `systems/display-driver.md` | intro 15, "Title tick art"; display-driver 8 | "The 50-row band pitch is a driver constant rather than a record height, so on **that path** the last source row of band `3` is simply background" — generalising the 50-row pitch to the whole `.4` (CGA / Hercules / Tandy) path. | The pitch is **not** 50 on every backend. The four-colour (CGA) path does keep the 50-row pitch, 49 copied rows and destination row 65 of the EGA baseline; the **Hercules** driver uses its own band geometry, published in `systems/display-driver.md` section 8, "Per-driver title-band geometry". An engine on the old text stages the four title-tick frames at the wrong band pitch on Hercules and reads the wrong source rows. R048, R050, R082 and R169 name display-driver 8 for other claims; none states the pitch. |
| R250 | `e0b73e1` -> `31d331c` | `systems/town-mode.md`, `formats/location-dat.md` | town-mode 3, former "Harvest scatter on farmland and orchards"; location-dat 6 | An **unconditional** depletion pass, published under the heading "Harvest scatter on farmland and orchards" and "gated on the player's actor slot already being assigned, so it does not run on a bare map load", walking the live floor buffer of **every** town-family location and knocking standing crops down to plowed patches and fruit trees down to hollow stumps seven times in eight, so that "on any given visit roughly one farm cell in eight is still bearing and roughly one orchard cell in eight still has its tree" everywhere in the world. | The pass is **gated on the entry-time resident-Shadowlord record**. In any location that is not currently one of the three Shadowlord hideouts, and in a hideout whose Shadowlord is already vanquished, it returns immediately and the authored farmland is drawn exactly as shipped; **only the settlement hosting a live Shadowlord is blighted**. The actor-slot precondition is also withdrawn: the helper that reads the slot is the Shadowlord install, and the section 5 row-`4` guard suppresses the blight for the same reason it suppresses the install. Two call sites invoke the pass (end of every floor load; first step of the Shadowlord install), so an in-town floor reload after an NPC death can run it twice, leaving a standing fraction smaller than a single seven-in-eight rule predicts. This reversal left **no retraction word** in either document; both sections now carry an inline note. |
| R251 | `d80c16b` (finalising `4ae70dc`) | `systems/view.md` | 4, view-class renderer table, class `0xC` | View class `0xC` has source bank "none": a table-mapped **no-op** / default class for tile id `0x01` that falls through without a dedicated renderer. Deep water (tile id `0x01`) is its sole member; it draws a single micro-blit at cell (2,2) and nothing else, taking the peer/blue family under peer/gem view and the normal terrain family otherwise, and `0xC` is now listed in both source-family rows. | R252 | `d80c16b` (finalising `4ae70dc`) | `systems/view.md` | 4, view-class renderer table, class `0x10` | View class `0x10` is the **fence/wall** renderer: a centre fill of cells `(1,1)..(2,2)`, edge bits adding top/right/bottom/left stubs, and tile ids `0x22..0x25` adding one **interior orientation marker** at `(1,2)`, `(1,1)`, `(2,1)` or `(2,2)` respectively. Source banks were given as frame-fill and frame-line only. | It is the **road** class — its seven tile ids are the whole road family and nothing else maps there. The cell opens with the class-`1` sparse checker, a secondary-terrain draw the old recipe omitted entirely, before the centre fill, which is the road body; a per-tile four-bit connection mask adds the edge stubs, with the shipped masks enumerated per tile id. For the four elbow ids only, the final stamp is a **blank-source** micro-cell over the quarter of the centre fill diagonally opposite the elbow, rounding the unused corner off the road body — it erases, it does not add an orientation mark. Both the class's identity and its draw recipe changed, and the old recipe also names the wrong source bank. |
| R253 | issue #89 (correction published `b3c1f4e`) | `systems/traps.md` | 4, surface/town combat-scene cleanup | When a container trap kills the chosen member, the surface/town site "removes that member from the live combatant records" and "blanks the associated world-object entry". | The combatant record is **not** removed, cleared or freed — it stays in place with one additional marker bit set — and the world-object entry is **not** blanked: a specific constant (decimal thirty, written as two byte stores) is stamped into its leading bytes. The third step, clearing the active-character hint when it named the member, is unchanged. An engine on the old text frees a combatant slot the original keeps and zeroes an object entry the original stamps, a durable state divergence. **Sourcing caveat:** neither withdrawn phrase appears anywhere in this repository's prose history, so it was delivered through the issue-89 thread; read that whole answer, not only the part quoted here. |
| R254 | issue #89 (correction published `b3c1f4e`) | `systems/traps.md` | 4, persisted trap flag; restated in 5's closed-gap bullet | Published as an **open gap** on the premise that "the surface/town container handler masks the trap bit out of its own working copy of the object's status byte only" and "does not rewrite" the persisted record, leaving open whether a trapped surface or town container can **re-spring** on a second Open. | The premise is false. The handler **does** rewrite the record: it zeroes the record's leading fields between choosing the acting member and testing the trap condition, after taking its own local copy of the byte, which is why the trap still fires on that open. The clear survives save and reload, a later Open of the same square matches no container and reports nothing to open, so a trapped surface or town container **cannot** re-spring. Section 5's gap list records the item closed. A negative claim that turned positive: an engine on the published premise keeps the record and re-springs the trap on every subsequent Open. Same issue-89 sourcing caveat as R253. |

The two override cases do not consult status at all and are therefore not covered by it. An engine carrying the unscoped form suppresses container traps in combat-class scenes and whenever an active character is set, which the original springs. Same issue-89 sourcing caveat as R253. |
| R256 | `1a8be19` -> `242bc2a` | `systems/shops.md` | 10.3 | "**Two** pieces of shop state are part of the save image", followed by a closed two-item list: the party gold word and the inn registry. | **Three.** The queued shipwright delivery — the pending vehicle-acquisition class byte and the pending delivery coordinate pair of section 6.1 — sits in the saved band as well, so a vessel bought but not yet delivered survives a save and reload and is placed by the next overworld entry. | R259 | `a97b490` -> `55a5f09` | `systems/traps.md` | 4, town underfoot damage tiles | Tile `0x8C` is a **chair-family** tile: a downward floor transition, with Stonegate's chair scene as a special imprisonment sequence. | `0x8C` is not a chair. | R260 | `d80c16b` (finalising `4ae70dc`) | `formats/shoppe-dat.md` | 7 | The three shared random-bark rows are a shop-noticing bark, a **shared initial greeting** drawn from four `SHOPPE.DAT` records by the greeting dispatcher "called in greeting mode", and a farewell drawn by the same dispatcher "in farewell mode". The healer/sanctum kind is consistent, not anomalous, once both rows are read as exits. | R261 | `a97b490` -> `55a5f09` | `formats/tlk.md` | 10.1 | Published in this document, not only in `systems/conversation.md`: some dictionary entries are intentionally empty and act as **word-boundary sentinels** — on an empty entry the byte runner does not abort, it sets a "leading space needed" flag consumed by the next emitted character, "the format's way of indicating a soft word boundary". | Empty entries are **not** word-boundary sentinels and do **not** arm the pending-space flag. An empty entry emits the leading space and then queues the raw token byte itself as a literal character, which renders in the alternate font. The flag is armed only by a **successful** expansion, and is consumed and cleared by the next printable text byte — never by a following dictionary token, so a run of adjacent tokens yields exactly one space between words. The polarity is exactly inverted, and every rendered line containing an empty index differs. Same reversal as **R034**, whose bundle names `systems/conversation.md` 8.2 only; no row named any section 10.x of this document. |
| R262 | `46df660` -> `3ca17f0` | `formats/npc.md` | 8 | The `.NPC`-to-`.TLK` parallel sub-map mapping extends to the class `.DAT` file as well: the *k*-th sub-map of a class `.NPC` file corresponds to the *k*-th 2,048-byte block of the matching `.DAT`, so a location's tile pages can be found by computing `2k` and `2k + 1`. | Sub-map index *k* is a roster index for the `.NPC` and `.TLK` files **only**. A location's tile pages come from the per-scene base floor-page table (`formats/location-dat.md` 4.1); a location owns an authored run of one to five consecutive 1,024-byte pages. For 22 of the 32 locations the page run is not `{2k, 2k+1}`, and for 20 of them the entry floor's page is not `2k`. Same reversal as **R109**, whose Section column reads "npc 9"; section 9 is "Slot zero and the empty-slot sentinel", an unrelated section, and it was section 9 at R109's own commit too, so that pointer was wrong when written rather than drifting through renumbering. The retraction is in **section 8**. |
| R263 | `46df660` -> `3ca17f0` | `formats/end-dat.md` | 2 | `END.DAT` carries **page or paragraph-start markers**: a primary `{` marker at the start of each narrative page, and brace markers `{` and `}` inside each page as layout hints. | **No page markers of any kind.** `{` is a first-line indent advancing the pen fifteen pixels; the file contains no `}` byte at all; the one-record-per-window binding of section 4 is the only pagination there is. An engine that paginates on `{` presents several windows where the original presents one, and one that scans for `}` looks for a byte the file does not contain. Same reversal as **R124**, whose Section column reads "1, 3, 4"; section 2 is where the primary withdrawal note sits and where the normative "no page markers" contract now lives. |

R160 covers the dungeon-mode side of the same finding and names `systems/dungeon-mode.md` 8, 13.2 only. |

R003 names this document only for regeneration *eligibility*, and R229 covers the audio cue's confirmation prompt. |
| R267 | `818d0ee` | `catalogs/item-list.md` | 5.4, Ring of Regeneration | Worn-ring effects in combat run **only at seating**: "each living wearer gets exactly one regeneration tick, applied by the pass that seats the party at combat entry". | Seating **and** the combat parser's committed-action tail both send the acting slot through the worn-ring hook, so a wearer can take more than one regeneration tick per fight; a free refusal branches back before that tail. The 1-in-16 destruction check remains encounter-entry-only (R266). Same reversal as **R219**, whose Document column names `systems/combat.md` 12, 14 only. Early spawn slots may roll the base class's **companion class** from the per-class companion table in `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md`, and the substituted class then determines the tile. An engine that built a per-arena tile-substitution lookup draws the wrong sprite for every spawned actor. The polarity is inverted, so an engine built on this legend walks NPCs toward the player instead of away. Same reversal as **R206**, whose bundle names `formats/npc.md` 5.3 and `systems/npc-schedules.md` 9 only; this catalog's legend published the same wrong label, and R145 and R068 name npc-roster 2 for other reversals, so a section-2 audit driven by the index would not have surfaced it. |

The complete forty-row join — accepted plane and coordinate, narration class, exact continuation, name column, live-tile guard — is published in `catalogs/gazetteer.md` 5.1 under "Authoritative stock E-Enter narration join", with `systems/commands.md` and `systems/overworld.md` updated to match. A negative claim that turned positive: an engine that suppressed the name line, or hid it behind a debug flag, fails every E-Enter transcript. What survives of the prohibition is narrower — the bracketed diagnostic form carrying a level number and raw coordinates still has no counterpart. No row cited commit `8c2b200` before this one. |
| R272 | `46df660` | `systems/endgame.md` | 8.2, decorative title strips | The two decorative title-strip pairs are drawn **on top of the panel** before the prose. | The strips are drawn first and the opaque panel is drawn afterward, so the panel replaces strip pixels wherever their rectangles overlap. The explicit composition order in section 8.3 was correct; the old "on top" wording contradicted it. |
| R273 | `e3c6a0e`, `5b816cc` | `systems/dungeon-mode.md` | 8, energy fields | Sleep and poison used a current **HP-derived** save threshold and changed status only when a `1..30` roll was strictly **above** it; stepping into a field completed the move, leaving the party standing on that field. | Sleep and poison compare each independent `1..30` roll with current **Dexterity** and change non-Dead status when `roll >= Dexterity`, including equality. Electric contact is the movement exception: it reverses the attempted adjacent step back to its origin, then independently deals `1..8` damage to every non-Dead active member among the first six slots. |
| R274 | `e0b73e1` | `systems/magic.md`, `catalogs/item-list.md` | magic 8, shared timed-effect consumers; item-list 7, Crown of Lord British | Negate Magic and the Crown shared one undefined **"enemy-cast gate"** that returned "no spell", implying a spell-only decision with one caller-visible result; the item catalog repeated that abstraction. | There is no single such gate. The pair is read at three automatic-action boundaries: the **whole** possess/blink/summon-daemon hook returns unhandled and continues into ordinary attack/movement; the teleport arm is bypassed and continues into ordinary stepping; and a scene-resistant ranged/effect attack is silently aborted while its outer attack action remains consumed. Each is checked whenever that stage is reached, with no once-per-turn latch. The prior spell-only wording hid suppression of non-spell specials and teleport and could not specify whether an action was spent. |
| R275 | `5b816cc` | `systems/shops.md` | 6, tavern pricing; 8.5; 10.4 | The primary tavern round had **no durable inventory effect**, and paid drinks had **no traced persistent effect on party state**. | The primary letter is a round/meal: it adds one shared food unit per living member and can place food on an adjacent bare table. The secondary drink letter owns a visit-local successful-drink count. On the next attempt after exactly three successes, answering `N` to the barkeep's warning arms drunkenness at twenty-five and subtracts one moral-standing point before the fourth drink's affordability check. The Blue Boar's selected wines increment the same count. |
| R276 | `0aa59ac` | `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md` | 13, save-image hook | Listing the door tracker among saved fields and then saying loaded saves **resume mid-floor** implied that a pending door auto-close resumes from its serialized bytes. | Journey Onward initially restores all four bytes, but the town location loader clears the previous-tile/active byte before reading the authored grid, leaves X/Y/countdown inert, and never reapplies the transient open tile. The door loads closed and no auto-close is pending on the first playable turn. |
| R277 | `5b816cc` | `systems/shops.md` | 6.1, affordability check | The tavern's `Beat it!` short-funds refusal was grouped with purchase failures that **return to the shop main menu**. | An unaffordable ordinary Ale, Rum, or Stout purchase ends the entire tavern visit immediately after the refusal. It shows no `Anything else for thee?` prompt and no closing farewell, deducts no gold, applies no Falsehood surcharge, and does not increment the visit-local successful-drink count. Drunkenness and the moral-standing debit still remain if the player had already answered `N` at the exact-three warning, because those effects occur before affordability is tested. |
| R278 | `38cc753` | `systems/conversation.md`, `formats/tlk.md` | conversation 7 and 7.7; tlk control-code table and labels | Byte `0x90` was classified inside the `0x81..0x9F` **engine-control band**, and the format table said it was structural data, **"not ordinary printable text"**, without qualification. | The label scanners do treat `0x90 <label>` structurally, but the ordinary runner has **no `0x90` control case**. If reached sequentially, `0x90` takes the printable fall-through and queues a value that flushes as glyph codepoint `0x10`; the following label byte is processed separately as an active GOTO. No label-boundary flush or discard occurs. Shipped declarations are NUL-prefixed, so well-formed streams stop before this accidental path. |
| R279 | `ae2392e` | `systems/audio.md` | 8.9, drowning | The drowning damage loop **"exits only on a total party wipe"**, stated without a status-domain qualification. | Shipped gameplay writes only Good, Poisoned, Sleeping, and Dead, and within that domain the statement is exact. Save loading can preserve an imported outside-domain byte, however. The loop continues only while at least one Good, Poisoned, or Sleeping member remains, while its damage pass targets every non-Dead member. An outside-domain member can therefore survive the loop's exit; the next overworld loop head nevertheless selects the ordinary defeat/rescue path because it uses the same capability predicate. |
| R280 | `e87bd7c` | `systems/stats-panel.md` | 4, party rows | The status field was described as the character record's raw byte emitted verbatim **"as a glyph"**, implying that every imported byte necessarily paints one cell. | The row forwards the byte to the shared cell emitter. Shipped Good, Poisoned, Sleeping, and Dead values are ordinary glyph letters, but an edited or legacy save can supply one of the emitter's control bytes; such a value performs its documented presentation control, including style changes or a window clear, rather than painting that byte as a normal glyph. No gameplay field is thereby mutated. |
| R281 | `e87bd7c` | `systems/stats-panel.md` | 3 and 8, timed-effect slot | The timed-effect slot was said to be written through the **full-screen text window rather than the stats window**. | The refresh keeps the stats window active, positions its cursor at local cell `(6, 6)`, emits the cap/glyph/cap sequence, and selects the message window only afterward. The stats-window origin makes the visible result absolute columns 30..32 on row 7, as previously published, but cursor and style bookkeeping belong to the stats descriptor. |
| R282 | `a97b490` | `systems/combat.md` | 6.3 and 16, vanish-on-death | The vanish row said it sets an unspecified **"per-combat status byte"** to 2, runs a **fade animation** on the underlying terrain, and then performs a **post-turn flush**. | Value 2 is shared action-result bit `0x02`, normally consumed as duplicate-narration suppression and reset at the next action boundary. The visual is a blocking, unskippable 256-pixel direct reveal of the underlying terrain with 31 world-tick checkpoints, not a palette fade. The final call is a mutating party control/faint scan: it can clear control, narrate and sound a faint, remove one Sword of Chaos, apply sleep, redraw stats, and overwrite the shared result bit. It is not a flush. |

| R301 | `issue #179` | `systems/animation.md`, `systems/active-objects.md`, `catalogs/tile-catalog.md`, `systems/encounters.md` | animation 1, 6, 8; active-objects 8 and 12; tile-catalog 4; encounters 4 | Generalising R148's withdrawal of water/lava/fire from the frame-selector families into a positive negative: "**no water, lava, brazier or torch tile animates through this pass at all**", "Open water and lava-like terrain do not: **no water or lava family is animated at all**", "Torches, braziers, fireplaces and interior water are **static tiles; they do not animate**", and the framing of the game as having **two** animation layers. | Those tiles have no frame family and no selector — R148 stands and is re-confirmed — but they **do animate**, through a **third layer** the specification did not describe: a driver-side pass that rewrites the loaded tile artwork in place on every animation step. It rotates `0x01`, `0x02`, `0x03` and lava `0x8F` down one pixel row per step (period 16 invocations, wrapping in-tile), composites the shoals frame into `0x60..0x6F`, `0x34..0x37` and `0xE4..0xE7`, XORs pseudo-random noise into `0xB0..0xB3`, `0xBC..0xBF` and `0xDE` through per-fixture masks, and swaps row pairs in the banner and sail tiles (that last stage is published at **probable**, not established - `systems/animation.md` Section 12.5). Because it mutates the **shared tile asset**, every on-screen instance changes in lockstep and the mutation persists; the fire XOR is cumulative and never restored, so re-deriving frames from pristine art is not bit-identical. Contract: `systems/animation.md` Section 12. Independently confirmed by black-box capture of the shipped game. |
| R302 | `issue #179` | `systems/visibility.md` | 8, shared variant selector | The four-entry variant selector picks the first entry "**when the current active character's class letter is Tinker**", otherwise a uniform random entry. | There is **no character-class input** to this selector. The byte it tests is the single global timed-magic-effect code, and the short-circuit fires while **Negate Time** is active — returning variant 0 for every actor for the effect's ten-turn duration. Negate Time is the only producer of that code among all statically decodable direct stores. Otherwise the draw is uniform over the four-value range, taken **once per composite pass per actor** and never cached (normative, `systems/visibility.md` Section 8.1). The consequence that a merged sprite therefore **visibly** re-rolls on about three idle passes in four is published at **probable** (Section 8.3), because the step from "a fresh value is painted every pass" to "a person sees it change" was not observed. |
| R303 | `issue #179` | issue #179 interim answers (never in spec prose) | comments of 2026-08-31 | Three claims delivered as implementable answers on the issue before the verification pass: that the water composite combines every destination as `dest = (dest & ~mask) | (source & mask)`; that the mask tiles are engine-private assets, naming `0x70..0x7F` among them; and that the composite additionally feeds "two gem ids". | The composite rule is **not uniform**: `0xE4..0xE7` is composited through the **complement** of the same `0xD0..0xD3` mask that `0x34..0x37` uses, because the driver inverts that mask wholesale between the two composites and restores it after. An engine applying one rule renders that set inside-out. `0x70..0x7F` are **ordinary drawable terrain** ("strange walls"), merely reused as the river mask. *Amended 2026-08-31 by R314:* the replacement list this row originally gave for the genuinely private ids — `0x00`, `0xC0..0xC3`, `0xCC..0xCF`, `0xD0..0xD3` and `0x100` — is itself withdrawn. `0xD0..0xD3` are dual-role exactly as `0x70..0x7F` are, `0x100` is an actor-half atlas index rather than a terrain id, and only `0xC0..0xC3` and `0xCC..0xCF` are confirmed mask-only. The verified destination set is the three groups only — the two gem ids are not reproduced by the re-derivation and must not be implemented. `systems/animation.md` Section 12.3. |
| R304 | `issue #179` | `systems/display-driver-abi.md` | 10, dispatch offset `0x6C` | The red/green plane-swap mode of the loaded-tile mutation entry is "**used for combat-style terrain coloration**", and the entry is dispatched from three resident sites. | Those three sites are thin **trampolines**, not callers. The five real callers are the dungeon look/view code (twice: a save-eight-bytes-and-set-flag mode, and the plane-swap mode itself), the **per-turn clock advance** (the moon/sun phase painter, which runs once per game turn rather than at a scene transition), the post-combat restore (the restore mode, gated on the dungeon path's flag), and the endgame (the whole-tileset remap). Combat never selects the plane swap. Any reading of this entry as "scene transitions only" is withdrawn with it. | R305 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md`, `formats/cbt.md` | combat 3, wall and blocked tiles; combat 7, per-actor body step 3; cbt.md round-safety bullet | The round loop **skips actors standing on blocked arena tiles** - "an actor whose record places it on a blocked arena tile is silently skipped for the round", described as a defensive guard against bad placement. | The skip is a **restraint guard**, not a walkability guard: the loop reads the arena terrain under the actor and tests it against exactly two tile ids, the stocks `0x84` and the manacles `0x85`. Water, swamp, mountains, walls, force fields and every other terrain are outside the test, and an actor on them acts on schedule. New Section 7.1 gives the full contract for both cases. An engine on the old text freezes every monster in the two water arenas, whose sixteen authored monster cells are all on water. |
| R306 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md`, `systems/encounters.md`, `systems/rest-and-camp.md`, `formats/cbt.md`, `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md`, `EXTRACTION.md`, `NEXT-STEPS.md` | combat 2 ambush bullet and 5; encounters 2.2 ambush-flag bullet, 4, 6 and 13; rest-and-camp 9; cbt.md outdoor metadata table and boundaries; bestiary placement notes; EXTRACTION `BRIT.CBT`/`DUNGEON.CBT`, Encounters and Rest-and-camp rows; NEXT-STEPS "CBT runtime closure", "rest/camp ambush presentation cleanup", "CBT format-boundary cleanup" and "combat placement-shuffle cleanup" entries | The terrain setup helper's placement-slot shuffle is **dormant**: "the complete caller census has one caller and it always leaves the branch inactive", "that branch has no traced live caller", and rest/camp entry "uses neither shuffle". | The helper has **two** callers. The ordinary wilderness or town encounter leaves the shuffle bit clear (identity slot order). The **surface camp ambush** - overworld `H` Hole up - reaches the same helper only with the shuffle bit set and forwards it, so the fifteen-random-transposition permutation is live and observable: arena record 0's sixteen monster cells are all distinct and all on grass. The permutation is **not** a uniform shuffle and must not be replaced with Fisher-Yates. The camp route additionally **skips** the clear-and-seat pre-pass, so the party's arena coordinates on that route are not established and must be observed before implementation. |
| R307 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/item-list.md` | combat 5, seating the party, and new 5.3; item-list Ring of Regeneration row | At combat entry "a member wearing a Ring of Regeneration runs the regeneration tick **once** at entry", with the ring-destruction check listed as independent of and after the ring effects. | The vanish check runs **before** the member is placed and before that member's own ring-effect step; it draws one uniform `[0, 15]` and destroys the ring on the single outcome `11`. The ring-effect step then runs only for members whose status is exactly Good or Poisoned, and its regeneration arm is a **whole-party sweep** drawing one uniform `[0, 7]` for every member alive and wearing the ring at that moment - so two eligible wearers produce two sweeps of two draws. Any **closed-form** seating draw cost (in particular "one draw per wearer plus the square of the wearer count") is withdrawn: a vanish removes a wearer before every later count, and the triggering population differs from the swept population. |
| R308 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md` | 7, start-of-round setup | The round loop's housekeeping bundle - screen redraw, overlay refresh, flush, per-slot scratch reset, spell-cast flag clear - is **start-of-round setup**, run at the top of every table walk, and (as circulated on the issue) draws nothing before the first actor acts. | It is a **loop-entry prologue**: it runs once per entry into the round loop, the loop is entered once per encounter, and the sweep restart jumps back **past** it. Its very first action, before any actor slot is examined, is a **full world tick** - a variable, unbounded PRNG consumer whose draw count needs live capture. Exactly one such tick therefore sits between the last monster placement and the first actor's action. An engine on the old text both re-runs per-round work the original runs once and mis-seeds its PRNG stream. |
| R309 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md` | 8, `A` row | The shared arena attack helper "announces the actor and the weapons it is wielding (or bare hands) before the attack resolves", making the armament line Attack's own announcement. | That line is the **turn banner**, emitted at the start of every keyboard-driven combatant's turn *before any key is read*, so it appears identically whatever the player then presses. What `A` adds is `Attack-` and `Aim! ` per attempt, plus a per-item name line when two or three items qualify. The banner scans only the helm, weapon-hand and shield-hand slots and names only items with a non-zero weapon-capability entry, so spiked helms and spiked shields appear while ordinary helms, shields and all body armour never do; a charmed monster gets name and colon only. New Section 8.1. |
| R310 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md`, `systems/audio.md`, `formats/cbt.md` | combat 6, table sync sentence, 8 cardinal direction codes bullet, and 11 step 4; audio 7.4 blocking-tone census bullet, refusal table row and "means movement, not attacking" paragraph; cbt.md movement bullet | Combat movement uses a **step-or-attack** primitive: "if the cell is occupied by a hostile, attack instead", with an explicit "hostile actor at the destination: run the attack roll" case in the movement primitive. | **There is no bump attack.** A direction key is purely a step. Rejection - which includes both terrain the mover cannot enter **and** a cell occupied by a live actor, friend or foe - prints `Blocked!`, plays the 165 Hz bump tone, drains input and re-prompts the same actor at no cost. No to-hit roll, no `Attack-`, no `Aim! `, no damage. Attacks are reached only through `A` and its targeting cursor (new Section 8.2) or through the AI attack path. An engine on the old text lets players attack by walking. |
| R311 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md` | 9, movement fallback and teleport | Ordinary AI stepping "asks the surrounded helper whether all four cardinal neighbors are blocked" and, if no tested direction is legal, the actor's move is simply blocked for that turn. | The random-cardinal fallback performs **no neighbour scan**. It makes up to **four independent attempts**, each drawing one cardinal direction uniformly at random and testing only that direction, retrying on rejection, and commits the first accepted direction. Consequences to reproduce: a monster with exactly one open direction can still fail to move within its four draws; and when all four fail the routine still reports the action as consumed - unless the final draw happened to be the first direction tried - with zero committed displacement. |
| R312 | issue #178 | `systems/chargen.md` | 6, delta table paragraph; 7, stat assignment | The questionnaire's dexterity byte is the accumulated virtue-delta tally alone, with "a hard ceiling well below twenty for any of the three stats", so a fresh Avatar could have a single-digit or even zero dexterity. | The dexterity tally starts from the shipped seed record's dexterity of **15**, and every virtue's dexterity delta is zero or positive, so a questionnaire Avatar emerges with **dexterity 15 or better** and a combat phase counter of 21 or less. Dexterity still receives no floor of its own at commit. Single-digit dexterity is reachable **only** through the Ultima IV transfer, which does not floor dexterity. The parallel STR/INT zero-start statements were not re-derived and are now flagged unverified in Section 7. |
| R313 | issue #178 | `systems/combat.md` | 8, other inputs | Digits `1`-`6` at the combat command prompt select party member 1 through 6 as the active player, stated without condition. | Only while the input layer's numpad flag is clear. That flag is set by an extended scancode **or** by a typed `1`-`9` while either shift key is down **or NumLock is active**, and it makes the command reader remap eight of the ten digits to direction codes (`1` SW, `2` S, `3` SE, `4` W, `6` E, `7` NW, `8` N, `9` NE; `5` and `0` pass through). With NumLock on, a typed `4` steps west instead of selecting member 4, and inside the targeting cursor it moves the cursor west. The earlier answer that typed digits are inert as directions and that "the two namespaces never collide" is withdrawn with it. New Section 8.3; the underlying rule is `systems/input.md` Section 5. |
| R314 | `issue #179` | `formats/tiles.md`, `systems/animation.md`, `formats/look2-dat.md`, `catalogs/tile-catalog.md`, `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md`, `systems/encounters.md`, `systems/visibility.md`, `systems/view.md`, `systems/movement.md`, `systems/active-objects.md`, `systems/npc-schedules.md`, `EXTRACTION.md`, `NEXT-STEPS.md` | tiles 6.2; animation 12, 12.3 and 12.4; look2-dat 3 and 4; tile-catalog 3, 3.1 and the nominal 304..319 row; bestiary 1, 7 and 10; encounters 4 payload-domain paragraph; visibility 6 blocking table and its correction paragraph; view 4 view-class table; movement 4 query-family table; active-objects 8; npc-schedules 8 and 10; EXTRACTION `LOOK2.DAT`, `TILES.16`, Animation and Tile-catalog rows; NEXT-STEPS tile-catalog closure entry | Tile ids `0x00`, `0xC0..0xC3`, `0xCC..0xCF`, `0xD0..0xD3` and `0x100` are **driver-private assets that are never drawn**, identified as such because "their description-table records carry the shared placeholder `*`, which is this data set's marker for 'not a describable tile'". | Three separate errors. **(1) The list mixes two id spaces.** The atlas is five hundred twelve entries in two halves: the terrain half `0x000..0x0FF`, which map and arena grids address, and the actor half `0x100..0x1FF`, which an active-object byte reaches by adding `0x100`. The first four entries are terrain-half ids while `0x100` is an actor-half atlas index, and the identical numerals `0xC0..0xC3`, `0xCC..0xCF` and `0xD0..0xD3` are simultaneously published as the Orc, Ettin and Headless **actor bytes** in `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md`, `systems/encounters.md` and `systems/movement.md` — which draw atlas `0x1C0..0x1C3`, `0x1CC..0x1CF` and `0x1D0..0x1D3`. Both claims were true at once; neither said which space it indexed. An engine conflating them renders monsters as stencils and stencils as monsters. **(2) `0xD0..0xD3` are drawn.** All four are placed as arena terrain in the shipped dungeon-room arena bank, and `0xD1` and `0xD2` are placed on both shipped Ararat pages of the keep location file, where the two complementary wedges form the prow of the wrecked ship; the resident view-class table gives all four an ordinary renderer while giving `0x00`, `0xC0..0xC3` and `0xCC..0xCF` the no-op class. They are dual-role exactly as `0x70..0x7F` are (R303). Only `0xC0..0xC3` and `0xCC..0xCF` remain confirmed mask-only, and `0x00` has no traced consumer of any kind. **(3) The placeholder proves nothing, and `0x100` does not carry the one named.** The terrain half's placeholder is a lone `*` on twenty-three rows and the actor half's is a lone `x` on sixteen; `0x100` carries `x`. Eleven of the twenty-three `*` rows hold cells in shipped map or arena data, including the telescope `0x59`, the sign and poster ids and the fountain `0xD8`, so "carries the placeholder" cannot be used to enumerate private, undrawable or handler-owned ids. |
| R315 | `issue #180` | `systems/main-loop.md`, `systems/animation.md`, `systems/input.md` | main-loop 9, world-tick secondary subsystems; animation 5, per-slot animator; input 2, idle mode | The world tick's active-object animator advances "per-frame sprite phases **and eligible ambient wandering**", and the animator's "wandering path covers ambient map creatures and similar actors that exist in the world table **between explicit commands**" — i.e. ambient creatures wander on the idle tick. `systems/input.md` section 2 said only that "NPC schedules and the in-world clock do not advance from this idle tick", which left ambient wandering ambiguous and was read by the other two documents as permitting it. | The idle tick steps **no actors of any kind**. The world tick advances presentation only — sprite phases, the tile-animation layers, the RNG dispatcher and the lighting timer. Ambient creature wandering and scheduled NPC schedule-walking both run in the **per-turn** path, together with the in-world clock. Reported and measured independently on the clean side: an adjacent hostile creature neither moved nor attacked across twenty-eight seconds of continuous idling, an in-castle townsperson took zero steps in thirty-one seconds, and date, food and sun readouts never advanced across one hundred and sixty seconds — while sprites animated in place throughout, and combat began on the first passed turn. `systems/input.md` section 2 is broadened from "NPC schedules and the in-world clock" to all actor movement, so the ambiguity that produced the other two sentences cannot recur. Two further structural facts published with it: dungeon mode runs its own idle loop with **no** world step at all, and the under-sail wait path takes **two** ticks per pass rather than one. The per-turn gate that decides whether a given actor steps is a separate question and is not settled by this row. |
| R316 | `issue #180` | `systems/animation.md`, `systems/active-objects.md` | animation 2 cadence sentence, 3 phase table, 4 per-slot pass case 4, 5 in full, 10 implementation rules, 13.2 step list; active-objects 8 phase table and the "Some slots also receive AI ticks" paragraph, 12 per-tick animator hook | The per-slot animator's phase-zero decision **moves actors**: "hostile creature classes that wander on the overworld (or in towns past their schedule) get an RNG roll on their phase-zero tick that **may turn or step them one cell**"; "some classes are gated by a random roll, so they **move** only on a fraction of eligible ticks"; "some **attempt a one-cell movement**"; "apply movement/collision rules before accepting any autonomous movement produced by the animator"; and idle-time "monster wandering". | The animator writes exactly two fields of an active-object record — the displayed tile byte and the packed phase/facing byte — and never the column or row, so **there is no animator movement path at all** and nothing for a collision rule to refuse. R315 withdrew the same reading from this document's closing paragraph and from `systems/main-loop.md` and `systems/input.md` but left these sentences standing in the bodies of both documents; this row completes that propagation. Search scope: the animator was read end to end and every record write enumerated, and every routine reachable from the world tick to a call depth of six — fifty-six in all — was swept for writes into the active-object table, finding only the renderer's mirror of the party's own coordinates into slot zero; the sweep did not follow indirect or far calls, and outside the animator and the renderer it did not chase writes made through a register-held pointer. Ambient creature movement is the outdoor per-turn walker's, and a town's loose horse-family objects belong to a separate town-mode object walker, so the "in towns past their schedule" clause was wrong twice over. |
| R317 | `issue #180` | `systems/npc-schedules.md` | 9, AI behaviours table, value `4` | The approach-and-attack AI "uses the wander step with a **shrinking range** around the waypoint" while the player is far away. | The wander radius is a **constant of three**, identical to AI value `1`, and never narrows. The distance this mode measures is used only to choose between wandering and engaging, and is then discarded; both arms enter the same wander step with the same constant cap. An implementation that tightened the radius as the player closed made bounded wanderers freeze in place near the player, which the original does not do. Section 9.1, published in the same change, now gives the whole per-turn wander contract. |
| R318 | `issue #180` | `catalogs/item-list.md`, `systems/inventory.md` | item-list 7.2 potion row 7 and the White visibility repaint sequence; inventory 620 potion family row | The White potion "invokes the ordinary visibility producer exactly once … with the value `32`", that value being "the inclusive squared-Euclidean gate `dx*dx + dy*dy <= 32`", which "admits 101 of the 121 cells before ordinary sight blockers and local-light rules are applied", with "a blocker inside the gate visible but stopping propagation past itself". | The `32` is an argument the visibility producer **never reads** — it is dead in both of that producer's callers — and the branch the White sweep actually takes is the producer's no-line-of-sight mode, selected by a negative sentinel in the light argument. That branch refills **all 121 cells** of the eleven-by-eleven window directly from the map with **no distance test and no blocker test**. The visible difference is large: walls no longer hide what is behind them and the window corners are revealed. The same sweep is the body of the X-Ray spell (*Wis An Ylem*), which is therefore a working see-through-obstruction effect. Scope, stated as an open question rather than an assurance: what was established is that the caller's light value selects the producer's branch and that the literal the producer hands its carve helper is hard-coded inside the producer and identical for every caller. Whether the caller's value *also* reaches the carve as a squared-distance threshold was **not** re-derived, and the routine that would carry such a test was not read. `systems/visibility.md` Section 3's positive-value row and Section 4's per-threshold cell counts are therefore **un-rechecked** by this work — not withdrawn, and no evidence against them was found, but not reconfirmed either. |
| R319 | `issue #180` | `systems/main-loop.md` | 9, closing paragraph on world-tick callers | "The world tick is **only** called from inside the input pipeline's idle wait." | Presentations, cutscene beats and paced turn loops call the world tick and its paced-pause helper directly, from dozens of sites across the resident image and the overlays; `systems/animation.md` Section 13 had already published that census and this document was not updated to match. What survives is narrower and is now stated as such: the *idle* pump is what prompt mode suppresses. An engine that can reach its world tick only from an idle poll cannot implement the paced presentations at all. |
