| R320 | issue #181 | `systems/overworld.md`, `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md`, `catalogs/tile-catalog.md`, `catalogs/gazetteer.md`, `NEXT-STEPS.md` | overworld 2 and 8 (falls bullet and the forced-movement table row); doors-and-z 10 "Overworld chasms" and 12 "Surface and underworld"; tile-catalog "Falls"; gazetteer 8.1 landmark table, 8.1 fixed-cell paragraph and 8.3 | The surface chasm has a "confirmed fixed trigger at Britannia coordinate `(54, 138)`"; the party "steps onto that chasm cell" / "walks onto it"; the fall "is a coordinate, not a tile class" and "an implementation must key the fall on the coordinate". | The trigger is the **waterfall tile family `0xD4..0xD7`**, tested in the cell immediately south of the party at the top of the input helper and under the party in the post-action pass, on **either** plane. The handler prints its banner and then **force-steps the party two cells south** with a world tick between the steps; only after the sweep, the marker hide and the damage pass does it test the resulting coordinate, and only Britannia `(54, 138)` writes the underworld plane. Britannia has exactly three waterfall cells and therefore three brink cells - `(46, 90)`, `(100, 96)` and `(54, 136)` - of which only the last lands on the gate; the Underworld has 116 brinks and none can reach it, because no underworld waterfall stands in column 54. An engine on the old text fires the chain at **none** of its real trigger sites - `(54, 138)` is the landing cell the handler tests after the forced steps, not a brink - and misses the two forced steps everywhere. |
| R321 | issue #181 | `systems/overworld.md` | 8, forced-movement table, "Surface chasm/falls" row | The per-member fall check draws "one random byte `0..255`"; damage is applied unless "the member's Dexterity byte is greater than the roll". | The draw is the shared skewed closed-interval `1..30` roll (a uniform `0..60` halved with truncation, zero promoted to one - the helper `systems/combat.md` publishes), and damage lands when Dexterity is **less than or equal to** the roll. Idealised, the roll takes 1 with probability 4/61, each of 2..29 with probability 2/61, and 30 with probability 1/61; those fractions are an idealisation carrying about 0.16 percent modulo bias, not the exact realised law. A `0..255` reading makes fall damage nearly impossible, where a Dexterity-20 member is really hit about one time in three. This roll must **not** be shared with outdoor K-Klimb, which draws a flat `1..30` directly, gates strictly rather than inclusively (the member falls when Dexterity is strictly less than the roll, where the falls chain applies damage when Dexterity is less than **or equal to** it) and applies `1..5` damage. Also published with it: the chain has **no per-member narration** at all - the feedback is a stats-panel row flash and a rumble. |
| R322 | issue #181 | `systems/dungeon-mode.md`, `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md` | dungeon-mode 8, pit-family Search table `0x61` row; doors-and-z 8, dungeon secret-door bullet | Dungeon Search on exact pit byte `0x61` "reports a found secret door". | The line is `A pit!` plus one line feed. The found-door line `A hidden door!` plus one line feed belongs to the `0xD?` **wall** branch, a different arm. Every mechanical half of the `0x61` row - the rewrite to `0x60`, the deeper-level visit mark, the hidden redraw and the dissolve - re-derived unchanged. |
| R323 | issue #181 | `systems/dungeon-mode.md`, `systems/commands.md` | dungeon-mode 12 step 2; commands 6, dungeon Search paragraph | The unlit L-Look refusal is `You see: darkness.` with a space, and unlit dungeon Search "reports that it is too dark". | Both stored lines break after the colon: L-Look prints `You see:` line feed `darkness.` line feed, and Search prints a leading line feed then `You find:` line feed `darkness.` line feed - a *find* line with a blank row in front of it, not a too-dark message. **No too-dark literal exists anywhere in the resident data image.** |
| R324 | issue #181 | `systems/dungeon-mode.md` | 8, pit/secret-passage/bomb-trap Search paragraph | Dungeon Search "first prints the generic 'nothing of note' search preamble", and the failed `0x62` roll "reports nothing on the pit". | The unconditional preamble is `You find:` plus one line feed. `Nothing of note.` plus one line feed is an **outcome**, taken by the plain-passage class and by a `0x62` roll at or below the threshold. The `0x60` outcome is `Nothing hidden` line feed `in the pit.` line feed, with the break in the data. An engine on the old text prints the wrong string in three places and prints it in the wrong position in a fourth. |
| R325 | issue #181 | `systems/dungeon-mode.md` | 6.4, flavour-conditional extras | A `0xB?` wall one cell ahead with a non-zero low nibble "routes to the per-dungeon scenery-text handler, which is what prints the dripping-stalactite family of ambient lines". | That handler prints **chamber-name banners** - BOTTOMLESS PIT, THE MAZE OF LOST SOULS, THE PRISON WRONG, THE CRYPT, UPPER/LOWER CRYPTS, DEBTERS ALLY, DEEP/DEEPER/DEEPEST, MOTHER LODE MAZE - in display mode 0 with the runic font and an inverse-style bracket, at a per-banner origin, and the non-zero low nibble is the one-based banner index. `a dripping stalactite.` is an **L-Look flavour description of the `0xC?` class** and never reaches this handler. Two banner spellings rest on runic digraph code points that are published as *probable*. |
| R326 | issue #181 | `systems/dungeon-mode.md` | 3, cell-class table, `0xB`-`0xD` row | The wall row carries "one debug 'SPEC WALL ERR' sentinel at `0xD`". | The sentinel sits on the `0xC?` arm of the L-Look description switch, not `0xD`, and it is **unreachable**: the flavour-wall test intercepts every `0xC?` byte before that arm is examined. `0xD?` describes as an ordinary wall, sharing wording with `0xB?`. Both the class attribution and the implied reachability are withdrawn. |
| R327 | `issue #180` | `systems/visibility.md` | 3, producer-behaviour table, Negative row; 6, Stage 2 negative-threshold bullet and the end-of-stage state block; 13, boundaries paragraph | The visibility producer's negative-light **full-fill** branch is "structurally unreachable in the shipped 2D pipeline", so that "no shipped scene drives it" and "a compatibility implementation can preserve the branch without treating it as ordinary gameplay visibility". | The branch is live gameplay behaviour. The claim about the *redraw orchestrator* stands — it zero-extends the unsigned lighting byte and can never present a negative value — but the orchestrator is not the producer's only caller: the spell/potion visibility sweep calls the producer directly with a negative sentinel in the light argument, and that is the entire mechanism of the White potion and of the X-Ray spell (*Wis An Ylem*). Both therefore reveal all 121 cells of the window straight from the map, through walls, with no distance test. An engine that implemented the branch as dead compatibility code has no working White potion and no working X-Ray. Filed together with R318, which withdraws the distance-threshold reading of the same effect. Scope: the sweep's two callers were found by a census of direct near calls across the whole shipped code set; indirect and far forms were not enumerated. |
| R328 | `issue #180` | `systems/town-mode.md`, `systems/commands.md` | town-mode 7, per-turn loop step 4; commands 3, town status bullet | The town turn loop "normally calls the NPC schedule processor with the current hour byte", and "**the sole exception** is the explicit-T arrest-cleanup result". | There are four ways the processor is skipped, not one. Three effect gates are tested ahead of both town walkers — the party's transport marker, then Negate Time, then Quickness — and each can skip the schedule processor *and* the town object walker for the turn; Negate Time stops all town movement outright while it is active, and the other two skip on alternate turns. The arrest-cleanup result is the fourth, and it is the only one of the four that skips the processor alone, because it is tested after the object walker has already made its pass. The same step also omitted the town object walker from its ordering: the walker runs between the slot-zero coordinate copy and the schedule processor, so a repaint conditioned on the processor's report alone misses objects the walker moved. `systems/npc-schedules.md` Section 5 owns the gates and the order; `systems/encounters.md` Section 2.1 owns the outdoor order, which is the reverse. `systems/commands.md` Section 3 carried the same implication in its town status bullet — status `1` "runs the epilogue *including* the NPC schedule step", with no mention of the gates or of the object walker — and now carries the qualifier. |
| R329 | `issue #182` | `systems/visibility.md`, `systems/combat.md` | 8.1, normative box and closing sentence; 8, terrain-substitution table; combat 5.3, step 6 | The four-entry variant "is drawn **once per composite pass, per actor**", and "There is exactly **one draw per qualifying actor per pass**", with what *qualifies* left to be inferred from a Section 8 table whose non-selecting fall-through rows read like ordinary rows. | **Only five rows of that table reach the selector**, and the two chair rows are far narrower than "a chair next to a table": each is gated on a *neighbouring-row* terrain byte that must be one of the three **laden-table** ids, and the accepted set **differs per facing** (the `0x92` chair accepts `0x9A`/`0x9C` below and rejects `0x9B`; the `0x90` chair accepts `0x9B`/`0x9C` above and rejects `0x9A`). Plain tables `0x94..0x96`, end tables, desks, candelabra and every other neighbour fall through to **one fixed occupied-chair tile that can never change**, with **no draw taken**. Roughly half the chairs in the shipped maps are fall-throughs, so **a seated actor that never changes tile is the expected result for the majority of seats, not a defect**. An engine that draws once per *composited* actor advances the single global stream when the original does not and desynchronises permanently. The bed row likewise stamps **one fixed tile** and takes no draw; it must not be implemented as a four-entry variant. `systems/combat.md` Section 5.3 step 6 carried the same over-draw, describing the mid-setup world tick's visibility arm as taking "a further uniform `[0, 3]` draw" per tick; arena terrain almost never carries a selecting row, so that arm normally draws **nothing**. This supersedes the "taken **once per composite pass per actor**" clause of R302's replacement text. |
| R330 | `issue #182` | `systems/visibility.md`, `systems/active-objects.md` | visibility 8, compositor branch list and Section 2 marker table; active-objects 12, Renderer paragraph | The compositor's type-byte `0x5C` arm is the "**Vehicle/avatar-family companion branch**", which "takes the special vehicle stamp" when the underlying cell holds marker `0x92`. | `0x5C` is **one ordinary NPC sprite family** (the tile-name table calls it a minstrel), not a vehicle or avatar marker, and the party's own type byte is the party sprite marker, which is **never `0x5C` outside combat** — so no vehicle and no avatar ever reaches this arm in a world scene. The `0x92` it tests is the **chair terrain id** standing in the grid cell, not a marker. The arm's meaning is that an actor of that one family seated on a chair of that facing keeps its own sprite instead of merging into an occupied-chair tile; off that terrain the slot falls through to the default helper with its frame byte **reduced by eight**, a remap recorded as observed and not explained. |
| R331 | `issue #182` | `systems/visibility.md`, `systems/prng.md`, `systems/combat.md` | visibility 8.4, randomness consequence; prng 4, idle-path consumers; combat 5.3, step 6 arm list | "**Two** per-pass consumers run on the idle path" — the viewport composite and the wind check. | There are **three**, and the omitted one draws **first**: the active-object animator draws from three separate points inside its per-record loop, ahead of the wind check, which is ahead of the composite. An engine reproducing only two diverges from the original stream on ordinary idle frames **even with no actor seated anywhere**. The corrected order and the per-consumer counts are in `systems/prng.md` Section 4, `systems/visibility.md` Section 8.4 and `systems/combat.md` Section 5.3 step 6, which had listed the arms in the reverse order. Scope: the inventory comes from a transitive call-graph walk over directly encoded calls; indirect calls and prologue-less helpers are not covered. |
| R332 | `issue #182` | `systems/visibility.md`, `systems/prng.md`, `systems/combat.md` | visibility 8.4; prng 4; combat 5.3, step 8 world-tick draw counts | The per-pass wind check's retry loop "**draws in pairs** until it settles, so its draw count per invocation is one, two, or an unbounded sequence above that", and `systems/combat.md` added "a pairs-based retry does not make every integer above two reachable, **so do not restate the count as 'any integer from one upward'**". | The retries are **single draws**, so the per-invocation count is **one, two, three, and so on upward** — every integer from one *is* reachable, and the combat instruction was exactly backwards. The first draw settles it in 63 invocations out of 64 and each additional iteration continues with probability roughly `0.15`. No maximum exists and an engine must still not assume one. An engine reproducing the pairs shape advances the shared stream by the wrong amount on every retry. |
| R333 | `issue #182` | `systems/visibility.md` | 8, Negate Time producer census | A census "finds **one** site that writes it as an immediate — the Negate Time spell handler", and the generic three-argument effect setter's "only three call sites anywhere pass the Protection code twice and a non-letter code once, **never this one**". | There are **two** producers of the Negate Time effect code. The spell handler writes it as an immediate with a **ten**-turn duration; the shared timed-effect setter writes it from its argument and **is** passed this code by one of its call paths — the **Negate Time scroll**, with a **twenty**-turn duration. An engine that freezes the variant selector only for the spell will animate seated furniture through the scroll's effect. `catalogs/item-list.md` has carried the scroll's twenty-turn install correctly throughout; it was this document's census that was incomplete. This supersedes **two** clauses of R302's replacement text: "Negate Time is the only producer of that code among all statically decodable direct stores", and "for the effect's **ten-turn** duration" — the freeze lasts ten turns from the spell and **twenty** from the scroll, so a reader watermarked at R302 must re-check the duration as well as the producer count. The *effect* identification in R302 stands. |
| R334 | `issue #183` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/item-list.md` | combat 11, shared to-hit helper; item-list shared to-hit summary | The shared to-hit helper "computes a score from attacker and defender combat ratings, `(attacker - defender + 30) / 2`". | **The operands are the other way round: the defender's rating is added and the attacker's is subtracted**, so the score is `truncate_toward_zero((defender - attacker + 30) / 2)` - the same orientation as the spell-resistance predicate in Section 9, where the target's rating is the added one. Combined with R232's (correct) `roll >= score` acceptance, the old text inverts who is favoured on **every** ordinary melee and ranged/effect attack in both directions: an engine on it gives a Dexterity-15 party member a 29.5 % chance of being hit by a speed-30 monster where the original gives 74.6 %. This closes the residual R232 published as "the attacker/defender labelling of the two operands feeding the score is now suspect and was not re-derived"; a reader watermarked at R232 must re-check the labelling as well as the direction. **The neighbouring combat-weight sentence survives and is confirmed:** the combat weight really is the defender term of the score in the ordinary melee case. What R334 withdraws is only the surrounding formula, which named the defender term the subtracted one. A consumer that grepped for the combat-weight sentence should keep it, not remove it. |
| R335 | `issue #183` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/item-list.md` | combat 11, the two stated limits on the to-hit draw; item-list shared to-hit summary | "The draw's own range is unverified: two private analyses disagree about which random source the helper calls, and it was not re-located in the pass that found the comparison direction", and **"No hit percentage is published"**; item-list called it "a uniform random draw". | The draw is **the shared skewed `1..30` combat roll** this repository already publishes for the drop gate, the resistance predicate and the Tremor/Poison Wind gate: one inclusive `0..60` draw halved with truncation, with a zero result promoted to one, so across the sixty-one underlying values `1` occurs four times, each of `2..29` twice, and `30` once. It is **not uniform**, and it is not a `0..255` byte. Both stated limits are withdrawn and `systems/combat.md` Section 11 now publishes the **idealised** per-score hit table `(2 * (30 - S) + 1) / 61` together with worked Bat-versus-Avatar percentages. That closed form is an idealisation, not the exact realised law: as **R321** already publishes for this same shared roll, the underlying range primitive carries about 0.16 percent modulo bias toward low values, so the realised per-swing figure against a Bat is 0.7457 where the fraction gives 0.7459. Parity comes from reproducing the helper, not the fraction. This supersedes two clauses of R232's replacement text ("the random source was not re-located, so its range - and any hit percentage derived from it - remains unverified"); a reader watermarked at R232 should treat the draw as settled. An engine that made the draw uniform over `1..30` is close but not parity-exact; one that used a uniform byte is roughly 30 % too accurate. |
| R336 | `issue #183` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md`, `formats/data-ovl.md` | combat 11 field-contact paragraph and 13 stat-record row; bestiary 1 row-field paragraph and the section 2 column header; data-ovl class-stat row schema and byte `+4` | The ordinary attack damage roller is the one "which randomizes attack value and defense"; class stat byte `+4` is the "attack-damage cap" / "Attack cap" / "Attacker-side maximum for ordinary monster attack damage". | **A monster's attack value is that class byte used flat, with no random draw at all.** Only the *party* side of the roller randomizes its attack value, and it does so from the readied item's `Attack max` (`catalogs/item-list.md`), not from a class byte. The column is renamed **Attack value** in all three documents. An engine that rolls `1..attack` for monsters delivers about **39 %** of the original's melee damage - a Bat brings exactly 6 every time, not a mean of 3.5. The *defence* half of the withdrawn sentence survives and is now stated explicitly: the defence term is an inclusive `1..rating` roll on **both** sides, and is skipped entirely - taking **no PRNG draw** - when the rating byte is zero, which is the common case for low-tier classes. |
| R337 | `issue #183` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md`, `formats/data-ovl.md` | combat 13 stat-record row; bestiary 1 row-field paragraph; data-ovl class-stat row schema and bytes `+0` and `+2` | "The tier and endurance bytes are the two class-side ratings the shared actor-rating selector returns into the to-hit and resistance scores", with byte `+0` able to "act as either the attacker or the defender term" and byte `+2` "used the same way as the tier byte". | The selector has **four** class-side arms, not two, and an ordinary melee or ranged/effect to-hit reads **neither** of these bytes for forty-two of the forty-eight classes. The rating returned is the actor's per-actor **combat weight** - the jittered class speed for a monster, the raw Dexterity byte for a party member - in every case except the classes carrying the `zero-selector stat row` trait, which supply the **tier** as the *attacker* term only. The **endurance** byte is the monster-side rating of the separate spell-**resistance** predicate. The **defense** byte reaches the damage roller directly and never through the selector; the selector's arm for it exists but no call site selects it. Neither byte is ever the defender term of an ordinary melee. An engine on the old text reads a Bat's tier of 5 where the original reads its jittered speed of 26-30, making trash mobs far less accurate than they are; the withdrawn "chest/encounter team-flip" reading of the same two bytes remains withdrawn from the revision before this one. |
| R338 | `issue #184` | `formats/saved-gam.md`, `systems/time.md` | saved-gam 5, calendar table rows `0x02DA` and `0x02DE`; time 2 derived-values bullets, 5 hour-tick bundle item 1, 11 persistence table | Save byte `0x02DE` is the "12-hour display", a "display-hour value derived from the hour and **recomputed on hour changes**"; and the pre-cascade hour snapshot at `0x02DA` is "taken at the start of **every** cleanup pass" and merely "used by the time cleanup to detect hour crossings". | The **value** rule survives unchanged - twelve for hour zero, the hour itself for one through twelve, hour minus twelve above that - but the word *display* is withdrawn: **nothing in the shipped game renders this byte.** Every visible twelve-hour presentation recomputes from the hour on demand. Its only consumer is the ambient-audio tick, which reads it as a count of remaining loud repeats and **decrements it toward zero on two of every eight of its own calls**, so a save taken any appreciable time after the last hour crossing reads `00`, not the live hour. An engine that stores a live twelve-hour value and never decays it diverges on essentially every daylight save. The write is also gated on the **saved-hour snapshot disagreeing with the hour**, not on a wall-clock hour boundary as such - and that snapshot is **not** refreshed by a mode-zero cleanup call, only by a time-advancing call (at the head of the path, pre-advance) and by the per-turn party-upkeep pass. The two together are why a stale snapshot fires the hour-change bundle once at scene entry with no turn consumed, which is exactly what the shipped factory seed does. Scope: the "no renderer" negative is established for a reference census over the shipped executable, all twenty-three code overlays and all four display drivers, covering direct, indexed and accumulator-relative forms; an access computed from a pointer base outside the scanned window is not covered. **How long the byte takes to reach zero in wall-clock terms is inferred, not measured** - no timing run was made and the world-tick rate itself is unmeasured, so no seconds figure is published. |
| R339 | `issue #184` | `formats/saved-gam.md` | 2, top-level region table row `0x02DF`-`0x02EB`; new 5.1 | Save bytes `0x02DF` and `0x02E0` were folded into an undifferentiated "Food gauge / mode scratch" band with no field identity, no value rule and no consumer. | They are the **cached Trammel and Felucca moon-phase digits** for the current day of the month, in that order, each stored as the printable character for a phase index `0..7` rather than as the index itself. The moon-phase status-strip renderer rewrites both from the day-of-month - before it decides whether either glyph is on screen - at every overworld and town-family scene entry and again from the clock's hour-change block on the surface and in towns. **They are gameplay state, not scratch**: natural-moongate transit selects its destination from these two cached bytes and nothing else, reading Trammel before noon and Felucca from noon, converting the character to a number and using it as the Moonstone slot index of `formats/saved-gam.md` Section 7.2, then warping the party to that slot's saved destination scene, X, Y and Z. An engine that treats the pair as disposable, or that re-derives the phase by a different route on load, sends the party through the wrong gate after a restore. Factory seed: both zero, replaced at the first scene entry. |
| R340 | `issue #184` | `systems/active-objects.md`, `formats/saved-gam.md`, `formats/ool.md` | active-objects 3, record-field table byte 6 and the "phase byte" paragraph; saved-gam 8.1, field table row `+0x06`; ool 4, record-field list item 7 | Active-object record byte 6 packs an animation phase in the low nibble and "a **direction-step counter that AI movement uses**" in the high nibble; the animator's rule reads as though a tile-class test guards the byte. | Byte 6 carries **no facing and no direction of any kind**. The high nibble is the slot's step within its **animation script**; the low nibble is a frame-delay countdown whose all-ones value is the freeze sentinel. A producer that writes a facing here is not byte-compatible - in particular the **player's own record carries zero in byte 6 in a shipped save, not the freeze sentinel**, and an engine that writes the sentinel there diverges on every save. The animator's gates also run in the opposite order to the earlier reading: the all-ones nibble bails writing nothing, a low nibble in `1..14` is **decremented and written back unconditionally, with no tile-class precondition, on any slot including slot zero**, and only a low nibble of zero reaches the frame-byte and tile-class gates. Slot zero's byte 6 survives a shipped save because of its stored **value**, not because the player's tile class protects the record. |
| R341 | `issue #184` | `systems/active-objects.md`, `systems/npc-schedules.md`, `systems/town-mode.md`, `formats/saved-gam.md` | active-objects 10 town/dwelling/castle/keep entry and 11 persistence; npc-schedules 13; town-mode 5 preserving-re-entry paragraph and 16 save/load bullet; saved-gam 12 | The town-family entry pass unconditionally "clears every slot except slot zero" and runs the roster load, runtime-state init and NPC reseat, with a preserving re-entry differing only in that "the active-object table tail is not zero-cleared"; the NPC runtime state "is *not* explicitly persisted as a chunk"; on load the engine "re-loads the schedule sub-block from the appropriate `.NPC` file, runs the same initialisation pass that runs on first entry, and recomputes the active waypoint from the saved hour byte", so "saving inside town does not freeze NPCs at their exact position; it resets them to 'currently scheduled location'"; and the `0x07B4..0x105F` band's contents "are transient for gameplay", so "a clean implementation may rebuild them on load". | The town-family entry takes an **entry-mode argument**, and **Journey Onward into a town-family scene passes the preserving mode**, which suppresses far more than the slot clear: the `.NPC` roster load, the runtime-state initialisation from the hour, the clear of records one through thirty-one **and** the whole NPC reseat. The tile load, dawn/dusk substitution, marker and beacon harvest, moon-phase strip, wind-banner reprint, resident-Shadowlord latch and mode-zero clock call all still run. This is **correct rather than a bug**: the entire NPC runtime family - schedule table, per-NPC runtime block, path queues and read pointers, type array, stuck counters - lies **inside the 4192-byte save image**, alongside the active-object table, so a save taken inside a town already carries its complete live cast and re-running the loader would discard every NPC's mid-route position, queued path and pursuit target. Loading therefore **freezes and resumes NPCs exactly where they stood**; it does not snap them to their scheduled waypoint. An implementation built on the old text fails in one of two visible ways: if it persists the band but reloads the roster, it loses all mid-route state on every resume; if it declines to persist the band at all - which the old text explicitly permitted - it resumes a **completely empty location**, and the location stays empty for any number of turns that create no loose object, because the per-tick NPC walker skips every roster slot whose type byte is zero and the type array was never loaded. The initialisation pass remains exactly right for a fresh entry, which is what every non-load path uses. |
| R342 | `issue #184` | `systems/active-objects.md` | 11, persistence paragraphs one and three | Active-object persistence "is byte-for-byte: empty slots stay empty, filled slots keep their current tile, coordinates, and animation phase", with the on-disk per-plane object overlay described as "a *seed* file, copied into the table on **first overworld entry of a new game**". | The round trip holds only **within a scene, between object-list reloads**. Every return to the overworld - from a town and from a dungeon alike, on every occasion and not only on a new game's first entry - reads the whole two-hundred-fifty-six-byte per-plane object list straight over the live table, replacing **all eight bytes of all thirty-two slots**, after which outdoor setup re-synchronises slot zero's first five bytes only. A natural-moongate warp performs the mirror operation, writing the live table back out to the per-plane file before the warp. Section 10 of the same document had carried the correct reload contract throughout; it was this section that contradicted it. Any statement anywhere in the repository that an active-object slot's bytes round-trip now carries the scene qualifier explicitly. |
| R343 | `issue #184` | `systems/moons.md` | 2.2, marker-painter below-surface bullet and strip-cache paragraph; 3, refresh cadence; 4, sources bullet | The moon-phase status-strip renderer "runs from **exactly one place**: the per-turn cleanup pass, and only when that pass observes the hour changing"; the painter's below-surface erase arm "is unreachable in the shipped game" because "the strip's only caller is the hour-change hook of the per-turn cleanup", so below the surface "nothing is drawn, nothing is erased, and **nothing is cached**"; and the strip's cached glyph bytes "are **not gameplay state**". | The renderer has **several** callers: every overworld scene entry, every town-family scene entry, the cleanup's hour-change hook (which alone carries the scene-and-floor gate), the two Blackthorn cutscene re-entries, and two command handlers that repaint the strip. Only the hour-change caller excludes a party Z with the high bit set, so the below-surface erase arm's reachability is **unresolved** rather than unreachable, and no negative about what is drawn, erased or cached below the surface is supported. The scene-entry callers are also the mechanism by which the cached glyph digits are refreshed on a **Journey Onward**, which `formats/saved-gam.md` Section 5.1 publishes as load-bearing for natural-moongate destination selection — an engine on the old cadence never refreshes them at scene entry. The "not gameplay state" sentence is withdrawn by **R339** in the same pass: the two cached bytes are persisted save fields at `0x02DF`/`0x02E0` and are the sole input to natural-gate destination selection. What survives unchanged is the separation this section was written to protect: the cache is **not** the saved-slot natural-moongate live-terrain schedule, and placement is still owned by `overworld.md`. *Resolved by issue #190:* the below-surface erase arm's reachability is no longer unresolved — the arm is **live**, on four routes, and `systems/moons.md` Section 2.2 publishes them and what the arm paints. The caller list above is also narrowed by row **R375**: only the first of the Blackthorn audience routine's two repaint calls paints on the cutscene path. |
| R344 | `issue #185` | `systems/text-output.md` | 4 | A window's width in cells is `bottom_right_x − top_left_x`; this figure is what wrapping decisions use, and it does not include the trailing column. A window whose corners are columns 6 and 33 has width 27 — twenty-seven characters fit before wrapping is forced. | Both corner columns are **inclusive** and the trailing column is written normally, so a row's **capacity** is `bottom_right_x − top_left_x + 1`. The 6..33 window holds **twenty-eight** characters; the twenty-ninth forces the wrap. `bottom_right_x − top_left_x` is real but is the *last legal window-local column*, an inclusive-endpoint budget the wrap and centring arithmetic carry internally — never a character count. The per-cell emitter wraps only when the cursor steps **past** the right column, which Section 5 already stated correctly. Consequence for every consumer: the gameplay message window holds **sixteen** characters per row, not fifteen. |
| R345 | `issue #185` | `systems/text-output.md` | 6, wrap accounting | The available width on the current line "is `window_width − cursor_x_at_function_entry` for the first emitted line, and full window width thereafter", published as the number of characters the printer accepts on that row. | The figure is correct as the printer's **internal** value but it is a last legal index, not a count. The characters actually accepted are that value **plus one**: `(bottom_right_x − top_left_x + 1) − cursor_x`. An implementation that used the published figure as a character count loses the last column of every row. |
| R346 | `issue #185` | `systems/text-output.md` | 6, degenerate long word | "If a single word exceeds the window width — a degenerate case — the original implementation overflows that word past the right edge before the next break forces a wrap." | Nothing is **ever** written past `bottom_right_x`. The collector never gathers more than the row can still hold. A chunk that fills the remaining row with no break byte to retreat to is kept whole, preceded by a line feed if the cursor is not already at the left edge, and printed from column 0; the next chunk continues on that row where it stopped. A word longer than a **full** row is hard-broken at the row edge into successive row-filling pieces. |
| R347 | `issue #185` | `systems/overworld.md`, `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md` | overworld 8.1 (the falls chain rendering, the wrap-width sentence and the "About the fifteen-column figure" paragraph); doors-and-z-transitions 12.1 (the dungeon-exit rendering) | "the fifteen-column gameplay message window", "the message window's word wrap at width fifteen", and the heading "About the fifteen-column figure". Both renderings were published under issue #181 in spec commit `4a8d4fa`. | The gameplay message window spans absolute columns 24 through 39 inclusive and holds **sixteen** characters per row. Same root cause as R344: `bottom_right_x − top_left_x` = 15 is the last legal column, not the capacity. **The rendered rows published in both sections are unchanged** — `Falling into underworld!!`, `Exit to Underworld!` and the town-exit chain break at the same points under either figure — so a consumer who copied the rendered tables needs no change; a consumer who implemented a fifteen-column window does. |
| R348 | `issue #185` | `systems/overworld.md`, `systems/text-output.md` | overworld 8.1, the "About the fifteen-column figure" paragraph; text-output 10.1 | The gameplay message window's rectangle "is **narrowed** exactly once in the whole program, by the intro overlay" and "after the intro's write nothing widens or re-narrows window two". | The intro overlay is the only writer of that rectangle, but it writes the message-window descriptor at **three** points with three different rectangles: once with the gameplay rectangle `(24, 11) - (39, 23)` and twice with transient narrow strips used by the Ultima IV character-conversion screens. Every path leaving the intro for gameplay re-establishes the gameplay rectangle afterwards and nothing outside the intro touches it, so the *standing* rectangle at play time is unchanged — but "written once" is false, and an implementation that installs the rectangle once at boot and treats the descriptor as immutable will not reproduce the conversion screens. The paragraph's stated scan scope (resident image plus twenty-three overlays; not the four display drivers; not direct descriptor writes) was accurate and is kept. |
| R349 | `issue #185` | `systems/doors-and-z-transitions.md` | 12.1, dungeon-exit mechanism sentence | "the plane name has no space to break on, and the printer therefore emits a line feed and restarts the word at column zero - so the word lands whole on the new row." Published under issue #181 in spec commit `4a8d4fa`. | The word is not restarted. With eight of sixteen columns left, the printer collects the eight characters that still fit, finds no break byte, keeps that chunk, emits a line feed because the cursor is not at the left edge, prints the chunk from column 0, and the **next** chunk continues on the same row at column eight. Identical pixels for this string, different mechanism: an implementation that special-cases "move the whole word to the next line" diverges as soon as a word exceeds a full row, which the original hard-breaks. |
| R350 | `issue #185` | `systems/encounters.md`, `systems/active-objects.md` | encounters 4, base combat class; active-objects, the base combat class / banner identity table | "The special case is the ship family: a masked sprite byte of `0x2C` ... The banner for this case prints a fixed pirate plural literal rather than the class-1 plural name." | The banner fallback is selected by **masked sprite byte `< 0x40`**, not by equality with `0x2C`, and the literal is exactly `PIRATES` (seven characters, uppercase, no punctuation, no line feed). The two readings coincide on the shipped Attack-command path, whose own gate forwards only masked `0x2C` or masked values at or above `0x40`; they do not coincide in general, because two of the four other entry paths into terrain-combat entry apply no lower-bound test. The ship family's **stat** class is still 1, unchanged. Implement the range guard, not the instance. |
| R351 | `issue #186` | `systems/encounters.md`, `systems/combat.md` | encounters 4, authored-placement paragraph; combat 5.4, land-over-water paragraph | "arena 14, for a party aboard ship, likewise puts all sixteen monster cells on deep water or water." | The all-water aboard-ship arena is **arena 11**, the one the selector table in the same section already assigns to "aboard ship and hostile on water". Arena 14 is the ship-versus-ship boarding arena, selected only when the hostile is itself a ship, and all sixteen of its monster cells are deck tiles. The selector table was never wrong; the prose sentence beneath it named the wrong record. Confirmed by decoding the shipped outdoor arena file: record 11's sixteen monster cells carry the two water bytes, record 14's carry the deck byte, record 15's carry water with party seats on grass and brush. |
| R352 | `issue #185` | `systems/combat.md` | 11, the Bat-versus-Avatar defence-draw table; 12, the zero-or-negative-result paragraph and the opening sentence of "Damage modifiers" | Three separate statements that a zero or negative damage result is narrated as a **miss**: the worked example's outcome-table cell "0 (negative; narrated as a miss)"; "The result may be zero or negative, and both read as a miss ... a negative result short-circuits with the miss narration ... The two routes are therefore gameplay-identical - a printed miss and no HP change"; and "Negative damage is clamped to zero and an 'attack missed' status flag is raised so the narration reads as a miss." | The outcome prints `<target> grazed!` with the rising action-snap cue, and it suppresses every later result line **and** the party stats-panel redraw that an ordinary landed hit runs. There is no miss flag: the shared result marker's bit raised here has exactly two writers, both this zero-or-negative condition, and exactly two readers - one prints the graze line with its cue, the other is a wider test (shared with the vanish marker) that halts the narrator before the kill, sleep, hit and wound chain and skips the party stats-panel redraw. Neither reader is a miss; the suppression comes from the second one. The mechanical half of the withdrawn text - same marker on both routes, no HP change, the experience block the only difference - is unchanged and confirmed. New Section 11.1 carries the full outcome census. Grep an implementation for a miss line emitted on clamped damage: with the shipped party defence byte of 7 against a Bat's flat attack of 6, two of the seven equally likely defence draws land here, so the wrong line fires on roughly two sevenths of *landed* monster swings. |
| R353 | `issue #185` | `systems/combat.md`, `systems/magic.md`, `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md` | combat 9, opening paragraph, Pass 1 closing clause, and Pass 3; combat 10, closing paragraph; combat 15, the spell-system hook bullet; combat 16, the monster special-action variants bullet; magic 9, "Monster spell-like effects"; monster-bestiary 4, "Shared AI And Reward Units" opening paragraph and the COMBAT actor-AI provenance entry | That monster turns "ultimately produce a *synthesised keystroke*", that "the AI generates the same bytes the player would press ... and the synthesised byte runs through the same per-letter dispatcher as a player turn", that the ordinary monster path ends in "the same command parser used by player turns", that monster turns "use AI command synthesis before they enter the shared combat parser", and - the load-bearing one - that "before the command runs, the AI assembles a one-line narration string - for example `<monster name> attacks <target name>, armed with <weapon>!` - by stitching together a short verb composer." The same framing was published in three further places, all corrected in this pass: `systems/combat.md` section 15's spell-system hook bullet, "Monster turns first pass through AI intent, target selection, direction synthesis, and the shared combat command parser"; `systems/combat.md` section 16's monster special-action variants bullet, "movement-vector synthesis, synthesized command dispatch, and parser reuse"; `systems/magic.md` section 9, "then target selection, direction synthesis, and a synthesized command byte enter the combat command parser"; and `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md` section 4's opening, "Monster turns run through the same command-dispatch architecture as player turns ... synthesizes the equivalent command byte, and uses the normal combat command parser", repeated as a provenance label in the same document. | The automatic actor driver calls the attack, movement and special-ability helpers **directly**. It reads no key, synthesises no key, and never enters the per-letter command dispatcher; its complete callee set was enumerated and contains neither the command handler nor any announcement routine. **No string of the `<attacker> attacks <target>` shape and no verb composer exists anywhere in the shipped game** - an exhaustive printable-run scan over the executable, all overlays and all four display drivers returns eleven strings containing "attack", every one a fixed prompt, refusal, event line or command echo. The two sides share the to-hit roll, impact presentation, damage roller and result narrator, and join *below* the announcement layer, which is why an ordinary hostile monster prints no banner, no `Attack-`, no `Aim! ` and, on a melee miss, no line at all. New Section 11.1. Grep an implementation for any attacker-named combat line. |
| R354 | `issue #185` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/spell-list.md`, `systems/magic.md` | combat 6.1a, controlled-bit writer 3 (Conjure/Swarm/Summon); spell-list, the Summon row and the Conjure/Swarm/Summon placement note in the implementer notes; magic 8, "Summoning and conjuration" | "They are still placed through the ordinary monster placement path, so their class byte is the monster-side one and monster AI drives their turns - **the bit never hands a creature to the player's prompt**", and the Summon row's unconditional "through the ordinary monster placement path (hostile, AI-driven)". `systems/magic.md` section 8 published the same reading in its most emphatic form: "That bit is **not** a transfer of control, and this is the single most important thing to get right about these spells ... the new actor keeps the monster-side class byte and monster AI drives its turns exactly as it drives any other monster. Nothing routes a summoned creature through the player command parser, and the player never gets to move it." | The bit **does** hand a monster to the player's prompt. The self-acting test reports "self-acting" for a monster-side slot exactly when the controlled bit is **clear**; a monster carrying the bit is dispatched to the keystroke/command path and takes its turns under player control, printing the reduced turn banner (name and colon, no armament clause), then `Attack-`, `Aim! `, `Nothing!` on a cancelled confirm, and `<target> missed!` on a failed roll. The withdrawn sentence also contradicted Section 6.1a's own dispatch paragraph, which already said a monster carrying the bit lands in the party's group. Summon's rebound branch, which leaves the bit clear, is the only case in which the placed Daemon really is hostile and AI-driven. **R074's withdrawal is reversed by this row:** the original reading R074 removed - that summoned creatures take their turn through the player-command path - was correct for the dispatch half, and R074 should not be relied on for it. R247, which cites R074 as the other half of a two-part withdrawal, keeps only its combat-group correction. |
| R355 | `issue #185` | `systems/audio.md` | 7.4, the missed-attack bullet; 9, the silent-actions list | "The other in-combat roll site returns silently on a miss, with no narration at all", and the silent-action entry "**a missed melee or weapon attack**, which adds no sound of its own to the swing cue that has already played". | Both are true of the **melee** arm only. A self-acting actor's failed *ranged* to-hit scatters the impact into the three-by-three neighbourhood centred on the aim cell, excluding only the attacker's own cell, and reports whoever occupies the landing cell; when that is anybody at all, the ordinary hit chain runs against them with its full narration and its own sounds. The ranged arm is silent only when the projectile pass fails, the landing cell is empty, or it holds the acting slot. The same gap exists on the party ranged and thrown arm, where a scatter onto an occupied cell skips the miss line entirely. `combat.md` Section 11.1 states the carve-out. |
| R356 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md` | 8.1, the turn-banner sentence | The turn banner is "a newline, the actor's name, and - for a party-side actor - the clause `, armed with ` followed by the names of that actor's readied items separated by `, `, or `bare hands` when none qualifies, **terminated by a colon**", with no line break stated - so the next thing printed, `Attack-`, continued the banner's row. The same reading was published in the issue answers that section backs. | The banner is terminated by a colon **and then a newline**, and `Attack-` starts the row below it. The colon literal itself carries no line feed, but the turn handler emits one unconditionally between printing the banner and reading the command byte, on the first pass and on every re-prompt, and the prompt-window refresh between them makes no cursor move that could undo it. Section 11.1's per-actor narration table, which always read "a colon, newline", was the correct one; the two are now consistent. Consequence for a consumer: `Attack-` breaks to a new line after **both** the turn banner and the multi-item item-name line of Section 8.2 - an implementation that keeps either on the same row is wrong on both. |
| R357 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md` | 7, the combat-overlay shared-state paragraph | "Nothing else in this section owns shared state. The blink flag and the marker coordinates named above are read only by that same painting pass, and neither is saved." | The blink-flag half survives: one reader, two writers. **The marker-coordinate half is false.** The aim marker's arena X/Y pair is written by the Section 8.2 targeting cursor and read back by three further consumers as the confirmed target cell - the ranged/cast effect arm, the adjacent-aim arm, and the world-side Cast command's field placement. An implementation that treats the pair as write-only presentation state, or recomputes it at draw time, breaks the confirm path that consumes it. Section 7 now also publishes what owns the coordinate: the cursor seeds it on open, rewrites it on every accepted move, leaves it untouched on a rejected move, and never clears it; a separate gate byte, raised at cursor open and lowered at cursor close, is what makes the marker appear and disappear. |
| R358 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md` | 7, step 7, the middle hazard tier; 12, "Monster status/effect attacks" | The middle damaging hazard tier "runs the shared finalize hook and **raises the leave-combat flag**", and - the same byte named a second way one section later - the poison arm's shared party-status/damage helper "marks the status-applied combat feedback, **requests the combat exit cascade**, returns zero damage". | It requests a **party stats-panel refresh**. Nothing leaves combat on that byte: it has four readers corpus-wide, one at the top of each mode loop's per-turn entry point, and every one of them redraws the full party stats panel and clears it. No reader returns, breaks a loop, writes a scene byte or touches any combat-exit state. Section 11's own hazard table already said so for the same tier. The same byte is raised inside the party status/damage helper the poison arm reaches, which is why the Section 12 sentence naming it a "combat exit cascade" is withdrawn here as well; both sites now read as a stats-panel refresh request. An engine that recorded the flag on a contact record and wired it to nothing was modelling the wrong thing, not merely an incomplete thing - the correct wiring is a shared display latch owned by the stats panel. (**Narrowed 2026-09-12 by R489.** The four readers and the test-repaint-clear shape stand, but "one at the top of each mode loop's per-turn entry point" does not: the four drains sit at the head of each loop's command prompt, combat's once per acting character's first keystroke and town's only on its full-prompt arm. `systems/stats-panel.md` Section 2.3.) |
| R359 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md` | 7, step 7, the top hazard tier; 12, "Monster status/effect attacks"; 11.1, the census row for the sleep line | "A top tier that routes the actor into the same **petrify-style** special effect a Gazer's gaze uses"; "Gazer attacks have a separate **stoning-style** effect against awake defenders, and magic/effect attack tiles can also enter the same poison or stoning-style branches **before falling back to ordinary damage**"; and the census row "Target slept **or stoned**". | The effect is **sleep**, and nothing in the shipped game petrifies or stones anything. It also **replaces** ordinary damage rather than preceding it: the resolver returns to its own epilogue immediately after applying it, so there is no damage roll, no defence roll, no HP change and no experience credit on that arm - exactly parallel to the poison branch the withdrawn text compared it to. A party defender gets the asleep status letter, the descriptor's disabled bit, the prone presentation marker, a cleared active-player sentinel and a stats-panel redraw; a non-party defender gets the disabled bit and its record marking; both get `<target> slept!` from the shared narrator and no sound of their own. A party defender already dead is refused with nothing written. An engine that recorded "the branch was reached" and then ran ordinary damage was wrong twice: the damage should not happen and the effect should. Section 11.1's field table always had the behaviour right; only the naming in Sections 7 and 12 was wrong. |
| R360 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md` | combat 11, the two side-table paragraph; monster-bestiary 3, the section preamble | "The COMSUBS spell/weapon dispatcher reuses that same class-indexed byte as the monster-side damage/effect selector, with value `1` treated as the **zero-damage sentinel that routes into the cast/effect branch**", repeated in the catalog as "the spell/weapon dispatcher also treats value `1` as the zero-damage sentinel that routes through effect handling". | **The polarity is inverted.** Value `1` is the **melee** sentinel: the dispatcher normalises it to zero and takes the ordinary adjacent Aim-cursor/melee arm, and it is a *non-zero* selector that routes into the projectile/effect arm. `systems/combat.md` Section 8.2 already said this for the same byte ("a class reach of exactly 1 is normalised to zero, so it takes the fixed-range-one melee path"), and the two sentences contradicted each other. Both documents now also publish the two consumers separately, because they disagree above the melee value: the AI attack resolver treats the byte as an inclusive **maximum range** and routes distance one to melee, while the shared spell/weapon dispatcher contains **no distance test at all** and sends any higher selector to the cast/effect arm at every distance, distance one included. Do not publish or implement one merged rule. |
| R361 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md`, `catalogs/monster-bestiary.md` | combat 11, the ranged/effect per-class-metadata paragraph; monster-bestiary 3 preamble (the "Cast-like branch" column gloss) and 6, the closing sentence of the lesser-beasts table | That one class trait "can route an attack into a **cast-like ranged/effect branch** ... That branch prints the cast/effect narration, reuses the AI direction/effect dispatch, plays the ranged animation, resets the scene state, and consumes the action"; the catalog column gloss "Cast-like branch marks the separate class flag that can consume the action through the cast/effect narration branch"; and the negative "**no additional Gremlin-specific resource theft** or nuisance writer is promoted beyond shared closest-target AI, ranged/effect routing, and default damage/death paths." | Only "consumes the action" was right. The branch is a **food theft**, not a cast: it casts nothing, aims nothing, animates no projectile and resets no scene state. On a **landed** attack by the single class carrying the flag (class 25, Gremlin - the only one in the shipped forty-eight-class table), the engine draws one uniform value over four and accepts on three of them; on acceptance, if the party's food supply is non-empty, it prints `A <monster> stole some food!` on its own line, subtracts five from the food supply saturating at zero, plays a rising cue, redraws the party stats panel immediately - this arm calls the redraw directly and does **not** raise the shared refresh latch of Section 7 - and **returns with the attack consumed and no damage chain at all**. The prerequisite state nobody could find a writer for is just the party's food supply being non-empty, so the branch is live in ordinary play rather than unreachable. Two parity facts: the draw is taken **before** the food test, so it is spent even with an empty larder, and the branch sits **after** the to-hit roll, so a Gremlin cannot steal on a miss. The catalog column is renamed accordingly; its row assignment is unchanged. |
| R362 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md` | 5, "Arena-centre special" | "No shipped outdoor arena carries that tile at that cell, so this is **inert for stock `BRIT.CBT` data** and is documented only so a custom arena behaves the same way." | The arena-file half is true and is now stronger - **no shipped record of either arena file carries the byte anywhere in its grid**, established by reading every record cell by cell - but the conclusion drawn from it was wrong. The qualifying byte is painted at run time, not loaded: the dungeon room painter's underfoot-icon table stamps it at the arena centre for a chest cell, so the step is **live in stock play** whenever dungeon-room combat is entered while the party stands on a chest cell. Three paths enter the setup pass (outdoor terrain combat, dungeon-room combat, camp/Hole-up ambush) and only the dungeon-room path can present a qualifying centre cell. Two further facts are published with it: the auxiliary-byte rule is setup id one's draw-free three-times-level-plus-seven, written into the stamped object's **quantity/loot byte** (the sibling setup id two instead **draws** one to ten-times-level-plus-ten, so the two ids are not variants of one formula); and **the step overwrites the centre terrain cell** with the room's floor-fill byte, so an engine that leaves the grid "exactly as loaded" leaves a stale glyph that the restraint test, the step-validity predicate and the hazard pass all then misread. Confidence: **established** for the gate, the formula, the destination byte, the terrain overwrite, the three-caller census and the arena-file negative; **probable** for the identification of the icon class as the chest and for the runtime sequencing that puts a party on such a cell, which was not traced - so the reversal of the inertness negative rests on a probable positive. |
| R363 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md` | 7, the loop-entry prologue | The prologue bundle is "screen redraw, combat-begin overlay refresh, screen flush, **per-slot scratch state reset, and clearing the 'any spell cast this round' flag**." | **There is no "any spell cast this round" flag**, and nothing reads one. The byte the round loop clears is the **auto-close door tracker's saved tile** - the first field of a four-field world record whose others are the door's X, its Y and a four-turn countdown, and whose readers all live in the world modes. The clear is also **per slot**, at the top of every one of the thirty-two per-actor iterations, not a prologue step; the observable consequence in the original is that a door opened immediately before combat never auto-closes, because the tile field is zeroed on the first slot iteration and the countdown is never reached. Separately, the genuine per-action scratch reset is also per acting slot rather than a prologue step, and it is a handful of one-action presentation and effect tags - it is **not** the thirty-two-byte per-slot band, which is the incoming-attacker map of Section 10 and is never reset by the round loop, by combat entry or by combat exit. Delete any modelled spell-cast boolean. |
| R364 | `issue #187` | `systems/combat.md` | 7, step 6 | "A single function asks 'is this slot a player or a monster?' - for a player, control passes to the player command handler; for a monster, to the **AI-then-command handler that runs the AI synthesis path before falling into the same dispatch**." This is a residual instance of the framing R353 withdrew elsewhere; it survived that pass. | There is no synthesis and no shared per-letter dispatch. The faction helper returns a group id and the branch on it is the whole dispatch: a **non-zero** group goes to the autonomous driver, a **zero** group to the player-command driver. The classification is not "player or monster" either - a dead slot classifies as group zero, a monster carrying the controlled/charmed bit classifies as group zero and is driven from the player's prompt, and a party member carrying that bit classifies as group one and is driven autonomously (Section 6.1a, R354). Independently, the player-command driver's Sword-of-Chaos arm hands a party character to the autonomous driver for one action. |
| R365 | `issue #188` | `systems/visibility.md`, `systems/combat.md`, `formats/cbt.md`, `systems/active-objects.md`, `systems/overworld.md` | visibility 11, the Combat paragraph (and the Section 3 active-map-buffer bullet's *pre-composited* wording); combat 15, the Visibility hook bullet; cbt 4, the Rendering bullet; active-objects 1, the shared-table paragraph; overworld 4, the 1-KiB buffer paragraph | That "the post-pass (fog refinement plus active-object compositing) **skips combat scenes entirely**" and that "combat **manages active-object compositing through its own round walker**". The same model was published four further ways: `systems/combat.md` Section 15's "the **renderer** composites actors over those values", `formats/cbt.md` Section 4's "the **combat renderer** paints each visible terrain byte as a top-down tile, then composites actors over it", `systems/active-objects.md`'s "the **renderer** composites it into the top-down viewport", and `systems/overworld.md` Section 4's "the **renderer** composites from it" - the last of which is in the document that owns the buffer being described. | **There is exactly one active-object compositor in the shipped game, it has exactly one caller - the post-pass - and combat enters it.** The combat round walker schedules *when* a redraw happens by calling the shared per-frame world tick; that tick reaches the post-pass on every one of its paths, combat included. The combat overlay contains no compositor and makes no compositing call. What a combat scene skips is not the scene but five specific steps inside the pass: the slot-zero refresh and the fog refinement for the whole pass, and per slot the viewport projection, the floor test and the two range tests. Everything else - the compositor, its terrain-substitution table, the empty-slot and missing-sprite skips, both grid-cell guards, the three direct-stamp branches and the shared-stream draw accounting - is identical in the two modes. Section 8.1's existing sentence about the three per-slot skips was the correct one and is now completed rather than withdrawn. The renderer composites nothing in any mode; combat's one genuinely combat-only render block is a scene-gated **presentation** tail on the shared tile-painting pass (turn cursor and aim marker, `systems/combat.md` Section 7), downstream of compositing. Grep an implementation for a second, arena-scoped compositing path: one built on the withdrawn text will not reproduce the furniture merge, the companion-band encoding, or the per-pass draw accounting inside combat. |
| R366 | `issue #188` | `systems/visibility.md` | 3, the world-tile getter paragraph | The out-of-range location/dungeon-explore query "return[s] a sentinel byte address (a fixed location whose contents act as a 'you walked off the map' tile)". | There is **no dedicated sentinel storage and no fixed sentinel tile.** The out-of-range query is substituted, not failed: it resolves to the loaded floor's **southeast-corner cell `(31,31)`**, which is an ordinary cell of the live map buffer that every location load overwrites with that floor's own corner terrain - so the substituted value differs per floor and per scene, and across the sixty-four shipped location floors it takes six different terrain values. The substitution applies symmetrically to a below-zero and an above-thirty-one coordinate on either axis. `systems/commands.md`, `systems/shops.md` and `systems/town-mode.md` Section 15 have described the `(31,31)` aliasing correctly throughout; only this document implied a constant. The other two branches of the same helper differ: the overworld branch **never** substitutes (both axes wrap inside the streamed chunk window) and the combat branch performs **no range test at all** (`systems/visibility.md` Section 8.5). An engine that built a constant off-map tile returns the wrong terrain for the town boundary-exit predicate on every floor whose corner is not that constant. |
| R367 | `issue #189` | `systems/npc-schedules.md` | 4, the stuck-counter bullet; 5, step 7 | The stuck counter is "incremented every tick the NPC fails to make progress", and step 7's "if the NPC fails to make progress, the stuck counter is bumped". | **Exactly one event increments the counter: a queued route step refused by the per-step cell check.** Neither cap-zero route-recovery step of Section 9.1 can increment it — not the queued-route replay's recovery step and not the exhausted-queue arm's — because the increment happens *before* the recovery step runs and nothing afterwards inspects how that step ended, and the recovery stepper never touches the counter table on any of its three rejection paths. Two further facts the old wording hid: a **successful** queued route step **resets the counter to zero**, and the high value two hundred is reached by **assignment** after a failed replan, never by counting up. An engine that bumps on any refused movement roughly doubles the rate at which NPCs hit the greater-than-three forced-replan threshold, and — if it also treats that threshold as the only reset — never enters the high band by the route the original uses at all. Scope: a corpus-wide census over the shipped executable, every overlay and every driver, covering direct, indexed and pointer-forming references to the counter table; pointer-through-memory and block-fill forms are outside it. |
| R368 | `issue #189` | `systems/npc-schedules.md` | 9.1, the closing sentence of "The same stepper is reached with the cap switched off" | "When the NPC's stuck counter is non-zero the walker only re-plans on a **one-in-three** draw and otherwise ages the counter for that turn." | The rate and the gate stand; **the aging half is withdrawn.** The draw is taken only while the counter is in `1..199`, and in that band a **failed draw ages nothing** — it simply ends that NPC's turn. Aging happens only at `200` and above, where **no draw is taken at all**, and a counter of `0` re-plans **unconditionally** with no draw. The draw's form is also now published and is not what a mask model would give: it is a uniform range draw over `0`, `1`, `2` accepted on **exactly the middle value** (exact rate `10923/32768 = 0.3333435`, realised `0.3342` on the generator's dominant cycle), not a byte-and-mask draw like the one-in-two wander coin. A mask model consumes the same single advance and selects a different third of the stream, desynchronising every later roll. The high band is five consecutive still turns and the turn that **enters** it is not still — see R369. |
| R369 | `issue #189` | `systems/npc-schedules.md` | 14, the "Long-stall visual parity" bullet | The high band routes "through a last-resort stalled-actor helper around the two-hundred-tick band", and "treat any remaining work as presentation/helper parity only if later tracing shows a visible effect". | The permission to omit is withdrawn: the high band is **observable gameplay**, not presentation parity. The value two hundred is assigned after a failed replan; the turn on which it is assigned is **not** a still turn — it falls through into the cap-zero recovery step, which can commit a move and write the NPC's position; and the band that follows is **five** consecutive turns of complete stillness (entry values two hundred through two hundred four, the fifth pass both incrementing past two hundred four and zeroing), after which the **sixth** turn re-plans unconditionally with no draw. The five-turn count assumes the walker reaches the aging arm on each of those turns; two upstream conditions (the tick's heavy-work budget and the NPC's state value) can divert it, and a diverted NPC stands still *without* aging, so the stall lasts longer. The surviving negative: there is still no separate durable gameplay state, no persistent schedule mutation and no alternate pathfinding state behind the band. |
| R370 | `issue #189` | `systems/encounters.md` | 2.1, the sentence introducing the probe | The encounter probe "runs when the overworld animator's pendulum gates allow the epilogue to continue". | **The gates are not the animator's**, and the animator can neither observe nor advance them. They belong to the outdoor per-turn block: two independent one-bit parity toggles, one for Quickness and one for the transport marker, each with exactly one instruction corpus-wide that changes it, and that instruction sits **inside the gate it controls**. Consequences an engine must implement: a Negate-Time-gated turn touches neither toggle; a Quickness- or transport-gated turn **advances its own toggle, because advancing it is what the gate does** — a gated turn does not skip the alternation; and a Quickness-suppressed turn returns before the transport marker is read, so it does not advance the transport toggle. Separately, the "phase or countdown nibble" issue #189 asked about **does not exist**: the probe holds no state, writes nothing anywhere, and is a pure function of the party's plane, the tile underfoot and the hour re-evaluated against a fresh `[1, 30]` roll every turn. An engine that models a gated turn as skipping parity along with everything else freezes the toggle and suppresses either every later turn or none. |
| R371 | `issue #189` | `systems/timing.md` | 8.2, the under-sail paragraph | An "**under-sail auto-advance pass costs two ticks and one world step and never enters the command wait at all**", with the helper's entry described as "one scripted step-and-wait — one world step followed by one one-tick wait". | The world-step figure and the never-enters-the-command-wait clause stand. **Two ticks is a maximum, not a cost.** The cursor poll waits only on its **no-key** branch, so a pass entered by a keypress — including the same-direction key the loop swallows — pays **one**; and both waits are subject to the Section 8.3 fast path, so on a machine whose boot calibration is at or below the threshold a pass pays **none**. The entry step carries no wait of its own. Three things the paragraph did not carry and an engine needs: each wait pass is a **fully consumed game turn** (clock increment plus the whole outdoor per-turn epilogue — encounter probe, spawn and creature movement), the under-sail world step is **unconditional** and does not inherit the scene-band suppression the shared command wait applies, and the same-direction key is **swallowed**. |
| R372 | `issue #189` | `systems/overworld.md` | 5, step 3 | Under sail on the wind-driven cadence, the input step "does not read the keyboard at all: the input helper returns the cached sail direction instead". | The helper **polls one key on every auto-advance pass.** Three outcomes: no key runs the advance body; a key whose translated code **equals the cached sail heading is swallowed** and the advance body runs anyway (which is why pressing the arrow you are already sailing does nothing); any other key **ends the loop and becomes that turn's command**. What is true is the narrower claim that the helper never enters the *command wait* while a heading is cached. An engine built on the old text either buffers a keystroke the original discards or cannot break out of the loop the way the original does. |
| R373 | `issue #189` | `systems/npc-schedules.md` | 12, the "Active objects (sprites)" paragraph | The active-object animator is "run independently each render frame" and "draws the per-NPC sprites". | The animator **draws nothing.** It selects each slot's animation frame; the single compositor inside the shared visibility post-pass is what stamps sprites into the viewport, and it is the only compositor in the shipped game. This propagates R365, which withdrew the same "the renderer/animator draws" model from five other sections but left this one standing. The cadence half is narrowed rather than reversed: the animator runs once per world tick, and the original's world tick is driven from a wall-clock idle pump, which is what the "each render frame" wording was describing. Issue #189 asked whether that cadence is prescriptive for a turn-based frontend; the answer published in the same section is that it is **descriptive**, with two named residues (the shared random stream and one frame-byte coupling). |
| R374 | `issue #189` | `systems/overworld.md` | 6, the pendulum-gate paragraph | A "small *pendulum gate* sits **on top of the animator**", and the `Q` timing/state tag "lets the animator and encounter probe run only on alternate turns". | **The gates are not the animator's** and it can neither observe nor advance them; they sit ahead of it in the outdoor per-turn block and belong to that block. Each of the `Q` and transport gates owns a one-bit parity toggle and flips it when the gate is **reached**, so a gated turn advances its own alternation rather than skipping it; a Negate-Time turn returns ahead of both and advances neither; and a Quickness-suppressed turn returns before the transport marker is read, so it does not advance the transport parity. This propagates R370, which withdrew the same animator-ownership model from `systems/encounters.md` Section 2.1 - the document that owns the per-turn-shape table - but left this paragraph, in the document that owns the outdoor per-turn block, unchanged. |
| R375 | `issue #190` | `systems/moons.md` | 3, refresh cadence (and the caller list published in the replacement column of row **R343**) | The moon-phase strip renderer's callers include "the Blackthorn cutscene **re-entries**" — plural —, listed alongside the scene-entry callers and the two command handlers as sites that repaint the strip. R343's own replacement text published the same list as "the two Blackthorn cutscene re-entries". | The Blackthorn audience routine contains two repaint calls and **only the first of them paints on the cutscene path**. The first always paints, always on the erase arm. The second paints only on the routine's early-exit path; on the ordinary cutscene path the scene byte has been forced out of the renderer's range earlier in the routine and is not restored until after that call, so the renderer returns at its scene gate having painted nothing and cached nothing. The cutscene is still followed by a refresh, but it comes from the **caller**: on return the town handler runs the town entry pass, whose floor loader repaints with the castle scene and the below-surface level both in place. An implementation that wires both of the routine's calls, as the earlier list invites, paints an erase-arm frame the original never paints and refreshes the cached glyph pair one time too many. R343 is not otherwise disturbed: the rest of its caller list stands, and Section 3 now publishes the census as a table with its scan scope stated. |
| R376 | `issue #190` | `systems/moons.md` | 2.2, the day-index paragraph following the twenty-eight-day phase table | "There is no day zero, so an implementation should treat a zero or out-of-range day as a save-data error rather than looking up a twenty-ninth entry." `formats/saved-gam.md` Section 5.1 carried the same assumption implicitly by describing the two cached bytes only in their legal-digit range. | The original **does** look one up. The renderer's lookup is a bare indexed read — table base plus twice the day — performed once per moon, with no comparison, mask or clamp, so every byte value zero through two hundred fifty-five reads something and the painter caches it; and nothing validates the day-of-month field anywhere else either (two writers, both in the clock's own rollover; the save loader is a block read). Day zero caches `0xF0` and `0x80`; day twenty-nine caches `0x04` and `0x00`; days thirty through thirty-two cache zeroes. Neither pair is a sentinel — they are unrelated neighbouring data. This is load-bearing rather than cosmetic, because the cached pair is the sole input to natural-moongate destination selection and is converted to a Moonstone slot index by an unchecked subtraction, so a day-zero save offers slot one hundred ninety-two or slot eighty and a day-twenty-nine save offers a negative slot. The advice to reject the save survives, but only explicitly relabelled: Section 2.2 now separates the **descriptive** rule (reproduce the unchecked read for byte parity) from the **prescriptive** one (clamp or reject at load, not in the painter, and record the divergence). A consumer that implemented the old sentence as the contract rejects saves the original plays. |
| R377 | `issue #191` | `systems/combat.md` | 16.1, the round-walker bullet in the downstream-effects list | The round walker's group-0 arm "prompts only for an eligible selected party member, while a monster descriptor that control moved to group 0 **still synthesizes an automatic action**". | **The player-command handler prompts and never synthesizes, for any slot.** A monster descriptor that control moved to group 0 gets the reduced turn banner of Section 8.1 and **one real blocking keystroke read**, and the whole Section 8 command cascade runs for it - the cascade carries no monster branch at all; only individual handlers below it differ. The handler's one gate is the active-player sentinel, and it is not "an eligible selected party member": while the sentinel is unset - its state on combat entry and after `0` - **every** group-0 slot is prompted, party or monster; while `1`-`6` has selected a character, every other group-0 slot is skipped with no banner, no key and no action, the controlled monster among them. This bullet was the last surviving instance of the synthesis framing R353 and R364 withdrew elsewhere. **R354 and R364 are upheld in full and are not disturbed by this row**; Section 6.1a's dispatch paragraph and Section 7 step 6 already agreed with them, and this bullet was the outlier. An engine built on the withdrawn clause refuses the player's keystroke for that slot and fabricates a turn the original never takes. |
| R378 | `issue #191` | `systems/combat.md`, `systems/magic.md`, `catalogs/spell-list.md` | combat 6.1a, the first reader bullet ("The attack driver"); magic 8, "Summoning and conjuration"; spell-list, the Conjure/Swarm/Summon placement note in the implementer notes | That the redirected fixed magic strike applies to any controlled actor: "When an actor whose bit `0x01` is set takes an attack action, the attack resolves as a fixed magic strike instead of running the ordinary weapon cascade", with the adjacency requirement "if the target is further away the actor's turn produces **no action at all**". `systems/magic.md` section 8 carried it forward for summoned creatures ("when its turn produces an attack it resolves through the fixed magic-strike branch, which also requires the chosen target to be adjacent") and `catalogs/spell-list.md` for the same three spells ("it likewise redirects that actor's attack action into the fixed magic-strike branch, which additionally requires an adjacent target"). | **The branch is party-slot-only.** It lives inside the automatic driver's attack path and is reached from nowhere else, so only slots the round walker sends to that driver can take it - party-side descriptors carrying the bit: Sword-of-Chaos compulsion, monster possession, and a Charm accepted on a party-side slot (which the spell's eligibility gate admits only for the shipped traitor identity, or for a party slot that already carries the bit, where the toggle clears it instead). A **monster-side** slot carrying the bit goes to the player-command handler and never enters the branch: it attacks through the prompted path, where the same distance-one number is the **targeting cursor's range clamp** - the cursor silently refuses to move out of range, so a too-far target cannot be selected and no turn is wasted on one. Same number, opposite mechanism. This is the row to grep for: an engine that applied the withdrawn universal form to a controlled monster refuses that monster's keystroke and gives it one distance-gated automatic strike, which is exactly the behaviour R377 also withdraws. |
